#231. Nvidia created a weird bit of intrigue by not filing a routine SEC report

During Nvidia’s quarterly call on Tuesday, CFO-turned-interim-CEO Colette Kress dropped this little oddity: She said Nvidia wouldn’t be immediately filing its quarterly results report, known as a 10-Q, with the SEC. “We plan to file our 10-Q in the coming weeks.” And she offered no reason why.

The delay was widely noticed. Among the many comments on X about it, economic and investment analyst Edward Dowd called it a “red flag.” Nvidia has typically filed the 10-Q the same day it reports results.

The 10-Q offers far more detail about a company’s operations than an earnings press release, including sections that disclose “legal proceedings,” which include lawsuits that could affect the company; and “risk factors,” which cover other potentially business-impairing items.

The announcement of the delay was so unusual that UBS analyst Timothy Arcuri asked about it after the public earnings call. Nvidia told him the delay was related to whether the company would need to disclose additional information about its ongoing investigation into Huang in the 10-Q.

“The company announced that the 10-Q will be filed ‘in the coming weeks’ – later than usual. When queried on our callback, management indicated this was related to the ongoing investigation and whether any additional risk factors would need to be added to the filing,” Arcuri wrote in his research note. (Arcuri still rates the stock a “buy” by the way, although he did lower his price target to $220 from $240. The stock is now trading around $150).

Notably, on the same day that Nvidia reported earnings, The Wall Street Journal reported that the SEC is investigating Nvidia over whether the company’s disclosures misled investors.

So the mystery thickens: What will Nvidia disclose? We’ll have to wait and see. (Nvidia has not yet responded to our request for comment.)

The delay suggests the company may have more to say about its mounting legal troubles. Nvidia is now facing multiple federal investigations over allegations that its partners were selling restricted Nvidia AI chips to China. So far, the scrutiny has come from a number of congressional committees; the Department of Justice looking into Megaspeed, the company run by a woman with whom Huang had an affair and who is also suspected of being a Chinese spy; and now, reportedly, the SEC.

Additionally, Nvidia is conducting its own internal investigation into Huang after he admitted to having an affair with Alice Huang (the Megaspeed executive), and allegations surfaced that he also had affairs with other Nvidia employees.

There’s little doubt that Wall Street will scrutinize that 10-Q once it’s finally filed. The intrigue brings to mind the old saying: “Doctors bury their mistakes. Lawyers put theirs in prison. And accountants hide theirs in the footnotes.”

#230. Has the 'Parthenon puzzle' been solved? The long quest to reassemble lost eastern pediment

The depiction of the birth of the goddess Athena above the Parthenon's entrance is considered a masterpiece of ancient art. Now, a German research team has pieced together all the surviving fragments.

The 2,500-year-old Parthenon is ancient Greece's most important temple and is regarded as one of the greatest masterpieces of the High Classical period of Greek art.

Dedicated to Athena, the city's patron goddess, it towers over Athens from its position on the Acropolis — a powerful symbol of the city's importance.

The Parthenon had a lasting influence on Western ideas of humanity and art. The sculptures adorning its pediments, created under the direction of the legendary sculptor Phidias, are considered unparalleled examples of proportion and grace. They have influenced artists from ancient Rome through Neoclassicism to the modern era.

The east pediment above the temple entrance depicted the birth of the goddess Athena from the head of her father Zeus, surrounded by the Olympian gods. A total of 39 figures adorned the roughly 29-meter-long work of art. At its center stood a colossal Zeus figure, measuring 3.8 meters in height.

For centuries, scholars have puzzled over exactly what the arrangement looked like, as the pediment was destroyed during the Christianization of Greece.

After decades of painstaking detective work and with the help of modern technology, archaeologist Vinzenz Brinkmann and his team have managed to piece together all the surviving fragments of the Parthenon's east pediment.

Brinkmann heads the Antiquities and Asia department at Frankfurt's Liebieghaus sculpture collection. "We're very, very confident that we've solved the mystery," he told DW.

The results are now on display in an exhibition at the Liebieghaus titled "Athena, the Stage is Yours: The Parthenon Puzzle."

Only around 20% of the original marble sculpture's volume is known to have survived. The fragments are scattered across various European museums, with most located in Athens. The British Museum also holds numerous pieces.

Brinkmann and his wife, Ulrike Koch-Brinkmann, have spent more than 40 years working on this ancient puzzle, with so many of its pieces missing.

And they were far from the first to attempt it: "Already in the 19th century, many scholars studied the subject and developed well-founded theories based on their deep knowledge of ancient sources," Brinkmann explains.

In 1910, a study documenting the many fragments held by the British Museum was published. The research team was able to build on that earlier work, but progress remained slow for years.

Then, in 2014, a visit to the Hermitage Museum in St. Petersburg provided Brinkmann with a crucial clue. There, he encountered a Roman sarcophagus featuring a relief of Athena's birth. "That's when it suddenly clicked: They're all there!"

Nine such sarcophagi are known to exist, almost all of them in Rome. Each depicts a different section of the Parthenon's east pediment.

Brinkmann emphasizes that other scholars had already noticed this connection in the 19th century. "But we really seized on the idea and never let go."

The researchers also benefited from 21st-century technology: 3D scanners and printers. Brinkmann and his team visited numerous destinations to examine around 60 original fragments at different locations — traveling light. An iPhone was all they needed to scan the pieces.

Digitally fitting the fragments together led to another important discovery. "We know these figures we're putting together!" Brinkmann recalls thinking at the time.

The Romans had repeatedly made individual copies of all the sculptures from the pediment. "These copies matched the fragments perfectly. Suddenly, we found ourselves surrounded by old acquaintances we had never suspected had come from the pediment." This finally enabled the researchers to reconstruct all the figures and their original arrangement.

Using a 3D printer, the team created a 4-meter-long model at a scale of 1:7.7. For the exhibition, they also produced life-size 3D prints of the most important fragments and mounted them on panels depicting the complete figures at their original size. An animation brings the reconstructed composition to life, digitally assembling the fragments to reveal how the east pediment once looked.

Alongside works from the Liebieghaus collection, the exhibition features loans from other institutions, including the Vatican Museums and the Louvre. Objects from the Acropolis Museum in Athens had originally been expected as well. But Greece withdrew its participation after learning that the British Museum was also planning to lend fragments to the Frankfurt exhibition. The Liebieghaus ultimately decided to forgo the British-owned fragments, too.

There is a reason for this controversy. Britain holds a substantial portion of the Parthenon frieze as well as fragments from both pediments.

Greek authorities have been campaigning for their return for decades.

In Britain, the 56 sections of the frieze are known as the "Elgin Marbles," named after Lord Elgin, the British ambassador to the Ottoman Empire in Constantinople.

In the early 19th century, Elgin had sculptures removed from the exterior of the Parthenon on the Acropolis in Athens. They were taken to Britain with the consent of the Ottoman Empire, which ruled Greece at the time.

Britain maintains that the sculptures were legally acquired.

For years, the dispute remained deadlocked. Since the Labour Party came to power in Britain, there has been some movement toward resolving it. But apparently not enough to persuade Greece to lend artworks to an exhibition in which the British Museum might also participate.

The exhibition "Athena, the Stage is Yours. The Parthenon Puzzle" runs through July 25, 2027, at the Liebieghaus in Frankfurt.

This article was originally written in German.

#229. Democrats skip a vote on adding Brendan Carr’s adviser to the FCC

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Trump is stacking the FCC with Republicans, even as the Democrats boycott.

Trump is stacking the FCC with Republicans, even as the Democrats boycott.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Senate commerce committee Chair Ted Cruz (R-TX) began a meeting Wednesday meant to confirm President Donald Trump’s nominee to the Federal Communications Commission with a lopsided dais. To his right sat all 14 Republican members of the panel, including Senate Majority Leader John Thune (R-SD). To his left were about a dozen empty chairs — every Democratic member of the panel was absent.

Cruz said he’d learned the night before that Democrats intended to boycott the vote, though Democrats say they’d repeatedly raised their concerns about advancing a Republican FCC nominee without a Democratic one. “The administration has shown it’s willing to move Democrats,” Cruz said, pointing to Senate votes scheduled on bipartisan pairs of nominations to the Commodity Futures Trading Commission and the Securities and Exchange Commission. And even though the Supreme Court confirmed his power to do so, Cruz said, Trump has not moved to fire any Democrats from the FCC, as he did at the Federal Trade Commission. “This is an ordinary nomination to fill a vacancy previously held by someone in the same party,” Cruz said.

“This is an ordinary nomination to fill a vacancy previously held by someone in the same party”

But in statements and comments to reporters outside the committee room, Democrats said that the process has been far from normal. Senate committees often vote on nominations to historically independent agencies in bipartisan pairs. Confirming Danielle Thumann Severs, senior counsel to FCC Chair Brendan Carr, to become a commissioner at the agency would put the FCC at four total members — three Republicans and one Democrat, Anna Gomez. Gomez’s term has technically expired, though she can remain on the commission through next year unless she’s replaced. But Democrats fear that by confirming a third Republican to the FCC, Trump might finally fire Gomez, because he’d no longer need her present for a quorum to vote on key actions.

Democrats asked for Thumann’s confirmation vote to be withheld until her nomination could be paired with a Democratic nomination. One way to do that would be to renominate Gomez. But so far, the president has not offered any Democratic nominees for the commission. Democrats said they made their concerns clear to Cruz, and added, “This is not a problem we created, but a problem that the White House can solve.” The White House did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

In addition to the procedural concerns, Democrats worry about Thumann’s work as an adviser to Carr, and whether she was truthful about the matters she worked on. In a letter asking Cruz to delay her confirmation vote, Sen. Ben Ray Luján (D-NM) detailed his concerns. At the November 10th hearing where Thumann answered committee members’ questions, she testified that she had “not worked with the Chairman on any media-related issues,” Luján noted in the letter. But documents obtained via a Freedom of Information Act request show that Thumann was “looped in” on various media matters, and “helped draft Skydance’s commitments that facilitated the 2 FCC’s approval of the Skydance-Paramount merger,” Luján wrote. “She also played a role in processing informal complaints related to CBS 60 Minutes alleged news distortion and she apparently orchestrated the Commission’s response to consumers regarding the Jimmy Kimmel controversy.” The FCC did not immediately respond to a request for comment on the matter.

Even Cruz admitted concerns with Carr’s handling of Kimmel’s comments in the aftermath of conservative activist Charlie Kirk’s killing. But at Wednesday’s meeting, he said he “emphatically agree[s]” that Thumann had resolved any lingering worries about that. He added that his JAWBONE Act, which the committee previously passed, could directly address First Amendment violations by government agencies of either party. The bill would let individuals sue government officials if they illegally try to compel social media or broadcast companies to remove their speech. Sen. Jerry Moran (R-KS), who’d similarly expressed concern over the Kimmel incident, said he found Thumann’s responses on the subject “comforting,” including in a phone call earlier that morning where he said he’d expressed his “serious interest in how the FCC protects and promotes free speech, including free speech on broadcast.” On the call, he said, “she was able to satisfy me once again in her answers, including an explanation for her role at the FCC and her lack of engagement on this type of issue as the FCC made decisions.”

“If she was lying about this, what else is she lying about and what else is she hiding?”

Speaking to reporters outside of the hearing room ahead of the meeting, Luján accused Thumann of lying to the committee. “The questions that I have is if she was lying about this, what else is she lying about and what else is she hiding?” he said. Luján said he wants both Thumann and Carr to appear before the committee by the end of the year to answer lawmakers’ questions. Asked if he’d support using the committee’s subpoena power or even pursuing impeachment against Carr when Democrats regain power next year, Luján said his vote to confirm Carr as a commissioner under President Joe Biden is among his biggest regrets, and that while he hopes Carr will testify voluntarily, “everything should be on the table here to be able to get answers for the American people.”

When it came time to vote on whether to advance Thumann’s nomination for a final step of a vote on the Senate floor, all 15 Republicans voted “aye.” That gave Thumann enough votes for her nomination to advance to the Senate floor, where it’s expected to pass along party lines once scheduled. Still, the clerk listed off the names of the 13 Democratic committee members whose seats remained empty. There was no response.

#228. Palmer Luckey doesn’t deny that he’s a prepper

Defense mogul Palmer Luckey — whose $90 billion drone and weapons startup Anduril is flush with government contracts — is still the kind of guy who prepares for the worst.

During Luckey’s conversation with Andrew Ross Sorkin at The New York Times’ DealBook Summit on Wednesday, the billionaire implied that Americans should batten down the hatches in case there are rough times ahead. Luckey himself has taken steps to prepare for a potential cataclysmic event, he said.

The conversation took place against the backdrop of a series of recent large-scale outages — at Apple, at Microsoft, at the NSA, and at the Federal Aviation Administration — that have led to concerns that America may be under attack by cyberthreat actors. The government has said that this is not the case, but Luckey cryptically teased that there may be more to the story.

“Look, all the 4chan schizo posters are like 12 hours ahead of the mainstream media on this. You guys are going to find out what’s going on soon enough,” he said.

Sorkin also asked Luckey to expound on comments made backstage “about what might be a cyber war that seems to be going on around us.”

Luckey described a “mismatch” between the sophisticated AI-powered cyberattacks that exist today and “old, outdated systems that are not able to adopt technology as quickly as it is developed.” He added: “I think people are going to have to pick up the pace. It’s time to start running.”

“What does that mean? Are you a prepper?” Sorkin asked.

Luckey didn’t deny the characterization. “I own a missile base 200 feet underground,” he answered, to audience laughter. “It’s a funny joke, but a missile base is actually a pretty bad way to run from the apocalypse. Everyone knows exactly where you are, and they [bases] are designed to take a hit from a nuclear weapon, a blast overpressure event.”

However, he had some qualifications on the prepper label. “The point is, like everyone should have three days of water. Everyone should have three days of dry food. If you have prescription medication, don’t go refill it when you run out. Make sure you refill it 72 hours before you run out.”

“People used to be taught this in middle school, and now everyone who remembers that is treated like a nut,” he added.

It should be pointed out that preparing for the apocalypse is not necessarily a new interest for Luckey. In 2017, a person familiar with the billionaire described him as a “prepper” and the bunker that was broached Wednesday is one of several decommissioned Cold War era underground bases that Luckey has purchased over the years. In 2024, he revealed that his massive video game collection is housed in one of them.

#225. Anthropic prices IPO at $83 a share, above projected range

Anthropic has priced its long-awaited IPO at $83 per share, above its initial range of $70 to $78, signaling strong investor appetite for one of the companies at the center of the AI boom, despite market jitters following the departure of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, which shook confidence in the industry. The Claude maker sold 980 million shares in the offering, up from the 825 million initially planned, generating $81.3 billion in proceeds and surpassing SpaceX’s $75 billion record to become the largest IPO in history by proceeds raised.

At the IPO price, Anthropic is valued at roughly $1.5 trillion on a fully diluted basis, a significant increase from the $965 billion the company reached in May when it raised $65 billion in its last private market round, but below the $2 trillion it was reportedly aiming for several months ago.

Anthropic will begin its life as a public company on Thursday, trading on Nasdaq under the ticker symbol “ANT.” A strong first day of trading would signal that investors remain optimistic about AI adoption, despite recent volatility in AI-driven stocks.

The company’s performance is expected to set expectations for private AI companies, including Anthropic’s main rival OpenAI, which was last reportedly valued at $1.25 billion but has yet to announce plans for an IPO.

Although none of Anthropic’s venture capital investors holds more than a 5% stake in the company, the IPO is a massive windfall for its numerous backers. Investors who own approximately 1% of the company reportedly include Sequoia, as well as former Google CEO Eric Schmidt. Singaporean sovereign wealth fund GIC also holds about a 1% stake in the company, according to a person familiar with the matter. Other backers set to make billions are Iconiq, Menlo Ventures, Coatue, and Sound Ventures, a firm launched by Guy Oseary and actor Ashton Kutcher. Spark Capital, the only VC firm known to hold a board seat at the company (it was the first institutional investor in the company, leading its $450 million Series C round in 2023 that valued the company at $4.5 billion), also stands to make a fortune off its early investment in Anthropic.

Some of the Anthropic’s existing investors, namely Sequoia, Iconiq, Lightspeed, Accel, and Altimeter Capital Management, are so enthusiastic about Anthropic’s future that they are reportedly planning to buy billions of dollars’ worth of the company’s shares in the IPO.

Yet the investors’ holdings in Anthropic pale in comparison to those of the company’s strategic partners Amazon, which owns nearly 15% of the company, and Google, which holds almost 14%. A group of major tech companies, including AMD, Broadcom, Google, Micron, Microsoft, Nvidia, and TSMC Partners, have also agreed to purchase $26 billion worth of non-voting shares through a private placement alongside the IPO.

The company’s seven co-founders, including CEO Dario Amodei, each sold nearly 3.4 million shares in the IPO, according to the S-1 filing, bringing in about $282 million for each.

This post has been updated with the number of shares sold and IPO proceeds.

#223. The nation’s largest school districts consider ditching Google Search

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Google is trying to force Gemini on schools, and they’re not thrilled.

Google is trying to force Gemini on schools, and they’re not thrilled.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

New York, Los Angeles, and Chicago schools are considering Google alternatives after the company said it will disable Search for schools that opt out of its AI features, according to The New York Times. Starting next year, schools will not have the option to disable Gemini-powered features without also disabling Search. Chris Phillips, Google’s vice president of education, told the Times that, “Search and AI are deeply integrated,” and that schools that turn off Google’s AI features “will not have access to Search.”

Many elected officials, including New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani and Senator Bernie Sanders, responded harshly, calling Google’s behavior “extortion.” Mamdani had recently announced a policy that barred 2-K-8 students from using AI in the classroom. In a statement to the Times, New York City schools chancellor Kamar H. Samuels said, “If Google doesn’t reconsider their position, we will explore other search options.” Chicago’s public schools have also reportedly told Google that if it can’t provide a version of Search without AI, “the district will look to other search solutions.”

In a statement to The Verge, Google spokesperson Laura Breen said, “We hear parents and teachers’ concerns about students using AI to shortcut learning, and we share the same goal: help students learn. To improve Search for the classroom, we’re starting to pilot Guided Learning capabilities in Search this month to walk students step-by-step through a range of complex problems, instead of just giving quick answers. Next year, we’re also updating our approach to controls for schools to address feedback we’ve heard from educators, so Gemini app, Gemini Notebook and Search will be off by default until schools decide which services to enable. We are actively building these experiences in partnership with educators, listening closely to ensure our products support students and teachers.”

Educators are wary of AI’s effect on students’ critical thinking skills, and some studies have shown that students who use AI generally perform worse on tests. There are also concerns about the mental health effects on children who develop relationships with chatbots at a young age. But Google is deeply embedded in classrooms across the US, which rely on Google Classroom, Chromebooks, and other services, and going fully Google-free might prove difficult.

#222. 'Coalition of the Willing' meets in Berlin: Europe remains committed to Ukraine

The "Coalition of the Willing" supporting Ukraine is gathering in Berlin. One of the key issues under discussion is whether Europe will be able to play a role in future negotiations over the country's future.

Once again, Berlin is rolling out the red carpet, and once again the focus is on Ukraine: Around 20 heads of state and government, primarily from across Europe, are set to gather in the German capital on Thursday (November 19). They will discuss the future of the Eastern European country that has been under attack from Russia since February 2022 and is now facing yet another harsh winter.

Extensive security measures have already been announced for Berlin's government district, pointing to the presence of high-ranking participants. It remains unclear whether Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy will be among them. What does appear certain, however, is the attendance of chief US negotiators Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner, according to several media outlets.

The "Coalition of the Willing" is a group of 36 countries determined not to be deterred from supporting the nation under attack. Unlike other international forums, the group is focused not only on how to support Ukraine militarily and financially in the here and now.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Led by Germany, France and the United Kingdom, the coalition is also prepared, in principle, to help monitor a possible ceasefire within Ukraine itself and to contribute to the complex security guarantees the country would then require, should such a scenario come to pass. The effort dates back to March 2025, when the countries involved first met in London.

But what can the coalition realistically achieve? Stefan Meister of the German Council on Foreign Relations (DGAP) is not overly optimistic.

"The fundamental problem is that Europe has yet to put forward a proposal," he told DW. "There is no plan outlining what security guarantees Europe would offer, what troops it would be prepared to deploy, what measures it could provide to safeguard a ceasefire, or where it would be willing to make compromises."

Nevertheless, the meeting comes at a moment when some observers believe there may finally be movement toward ending the war. During talks in Abu Dhabi in the United Arab Emirates a few days ago, Russia, Ukraine and the US managed to agree only on a prisoner exchange. It was a modest step forward.

Further talks are planned, and it has been a long time since the prospects appeared as favorable as they do now for Europe to secure a role in future decisions on war and peace in Ukraine.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

It has repeatedly been argued, both by US officials and by those in power in Russia, that Europe does not speak with a single voice.

This has sparked a debate among EU heads of state and government over who, exactly, could represent Europe in such talks. Could it be German Chancellor Friedrich Merz of the center-right Christian Democratic Union (CDU)?

After the EU summit in Dublin, Ireland, on Friday, Merz said: "For the first time, concrete peace talks are expected to take place in the coming weeks with Europe represented. Germany has a seat at the table, that is absolutely clear."

But what exactly did that mean? That Germany alone would speak for all of Europe? Hardly. As was also made clear in Berlin on Monday, Europe would be represented by the E3 countries, namely Germany, France, and the non-EU member United Kingdom, joined by Poland and Italy. Official EU representatives, including European Council President Antonio Costa, would also take part.

Stefan Meister of the DGAP believes the debate says a great deal about the pressures Europe itself is under.

"Europe's positioning has more to do with domestic political considerations than with developments in the negotiations," Meister said. "Everyone needs to show that they are participating in the talks in some way. Elections will be held next year in France and Poland, and it is still unclear whether French support for Ukraine will continue in its current form after that."

German Deputy Government Spokesperson Sebastian Hille, meanwhile, sought to downplay the debate on Monday over a possible role for Friedrich Merz as chief negotiator.

At the federal government's regular press briefing, he said: "This is not about discussing individuals. What matters is that Putin finally understands that he must come to the negotiating table so that we have a chance of achieving peace in the first place."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

US negotiator Jared Kushner, the son-in-law of President Donald Trump, struck a rather different tone last week.

Speaking in Abu Dhabi, he said: "If Europe could identify one person to speak on their behalf, that would be a bigger achievement than getting the Russians to include them in the negotiations."

Government spokesperson Hille responded on Monday: "Europe's strength lies precisely in the unity and cohesion of many countries. We should not allow ourselves to be drawn into debates that others want to frame around this question."

Hille emphasized that the situation remained unchanged: Peace in Ukraine could only be achieved step by step. Europe, too, would need stamina, perseverance and a willingness to undertake the painstaking work required to bring it about.

European supporters of Ukraine are also under pressure as public opinion on the issue is shifting, including in Germany. While a broad majority favored helping defend the country in the immediate aftermath of Russia's invasion in the spring of 2022, support has since fallen markedly.

According to the latest DeutschlandTrend survey on the issue, conducted or German public broadcaster ARD in September 2026, 52% of eligible voters still support weapons deliveries to Ukraine, while 42% believe such support has gone too far. That is the highest level of opposition recorded since the start of the war.

A further study conducted for public broadcaster WDR in November 2026 found that support for Ukraine has become a more polarizing issue in Germany than immigration. According to the survey, 54% of respondents favor continued support for Ukraine, while just under 29% would prefer a rapprochement with Russia, despite its war of aggression against Ukraine.

This article was originally written in German.

#221. Satya Nadella predicts AI agents will reshuffle the economics of the web

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella predicts that the rise of AI agents is going to reshape the economics of the internet, impacting the business models of the companies that rely on advertising and what he referred to as “human web” traffic.

Speaking at The New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday, the tech executive argued that the shift to an agentic web could change which companies pay others for traffic and for transactions, similar to how the transition from web portals like Yahoo to search engines like Google did years ago.

“The current aggregators who have a business model which was for the human web will get, in some sense, reshuffled,” said Nadella. “And if you were monetizing ads because of the eyeballs, you now have to probably get down to essentially maybe CPC [cost-per-click] wins; maybe it’s more transactional,” he continued. “And so if you don’t accept the agent, somebody else will.”

The discussion turned to Amazon and its decision to block AI agents from accessing its retail website, preventing them from browsing its catalog and making purchases on behalf of their users. Nadella suggested that companies trying to protect their existing business models could lose out to others that are more willing to embrace the AI agents. For instance, if Amazon continues blocking agents, another shopping platform such as Shopify, could allow them, and gain an advantage.

“I think this is going to be interesting,” he said, speaking to the changing models. “It’s the same thing that’s happening in other categories, right? Which is, if you’re in love with a particular business model, you have to kind of scramble,” he said.

#218. Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella remains confident in Nvidia after Huang’s exit

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella weighed in on former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s unexpected departure amid a series of shocking controversies.

“It’s all sort of pretty tragic in the sense of, you know, Jensen is somebody I’ve known for a long time. I’ve admired what he’s done, and at this point, I kind of want to give him space to figure this out,” Nadella said onstage at The New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday.

Huang stepped down last week from his CEO role, which he has held for 33 years. Huang’s leadership has been central to Nvidia’s ascent to one of the world’s most valuable companies, but his judgment was questioned after it was reported that he had an affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, whom U.S. officials believe is a Chinese spy feeding information back to Beijing. It was also revealed that Huang was having affairs with two Nvidia employees, including one who was a direct report.

While the world waits to see what the long-term economic impacts of Huang’s departure might be, Nadella says he still has confidence in Nvidia, which is a close partner of Microsoft.

“I’ll just say this one thing,” Nadella added. “Colette [Kress, interim CEO at Nvidia] worked at Microsoft for a long time. I’ve known her for a long time. She’s a tremendous person… I’m in touch with the same set of people I was in touch with before [Huang left], so I have a lot of confidence in the Nvidia team and their ability to continue to do the good work.”

Microsoft is not unlike Nvidia in that its founder Bill Gates inhabited a larger-than-life persona as CEO.

“Founders are unbelievable in the sense that they create something from nothing, so that is pretty magical… They’re singular, but they’re not gods, right?” Nadella said. “I used to say to Bill [Gates]… Founders also die, and so at some point, a company, if it needs to succeed, it has to have someone take over.”

#217. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#215. Elon Musk says ousting Jensen Huang is like ‘kicking Michael Jordan off your team’

Elon Musk has already been vocal about his disapproval of the Nvidia board’s decision to oust its founder and CEO Jensen Huang after a series of extramarital affairs were made public, including one with an alleged Chinese spy who is suspected of feeding sensitive information back to Beijing. At The New York Times DealBook Summit on Wednesday, Musk elaborated on his thoughts about Nvidia’s leadership change.

“Jensen is an old friend of mine,” Musk said. “I would call Jensen, you know, Michael Jordan at his peak level, in terms of what he means to a team.”

On X, the social media platform he owns, Musk even said he would hire Huang.

“He’s one of the most talented entrepreneur-CEOs in history,” Musk said when asked about his X post. “Who wouldn’t want to hire Jensen?”

Musk also signed a petition, alongside over 200,000 other people, urging Nvidia to reinstate its former CEO. Nvidia’s board, however, confirmed it is not planning to bring Huang back.

“[It] doesn’t seem like a smart decision to me…” Musk said of that decision. “There’s no one that can really replace Jensen.”

Huang “has long-term relationships that have been built up over decades,” Musk added. “He understands technology incredibly well. His team loves him… and many of them are there because of him, and have higher offers to go elsewhere, and have turned them down because of personal loyalty to Jensen.”

“China wants Jensen out, so I want him in,” Luckey said earlier in the day at the DealBook event. “They’re thrilled to have Jensen out… There’s a reason it’s knocking a trillion dollars off Nvidia’s market cap. They know it makes America weaker to knock him out.”

Musk also did not express concern over what Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy could mean for U.S. national security.

“I don’t think he’s disloyal to the United States,” Musk said. “I think it’s like kicking Michael Jordan off your team when you’re on a winning streak, and it just doesn’t seem like a good thing for shareholders.”

Other peers in the industry, like Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, reaffirmed their faith in Nvidia to move forward without its founder and CEO of 33 years at the helm.

“It’s the Nvidia board’s decision, but I’ll just say this one thing,” Nadella said at DealBook. “Colette [Kress, interim CEO of Nvidia] worked at Microsoft for a long time. I’ve known her for a long time. She’s a tremendous person… I have a lot of confidence in the Nvidia team and their ability to continue to do the good work.”

#214. North Korea expanded uranium enrichment plant, says IAEA head

IAEA chief Rafael Grossi has said the ongoing expansion of North Korea's uranium enrichment program remains "a cause for serious concern."

United Nations (UN) nuclear watchdog chief Rafael Grossi said on Wednesday North Korea has built an extension on its original uranium-enrichment plant at the Yongbyon nuclear complex.

The International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) has been unable to access North Korea since Pyongyang expelled its inspectors in 2009. It now relies primarily on satellite imagery to monitor the country's nuclear activity.

Grossi addressed the ongoing operation of enrichment facilities at Yongbyon and Kangson during a quarterly meeting of the IAEA's Board of Governors.

Kangson lies just southwest of Pyongyang, while the much larger Yongbyon facility is located farther north of the capital.

"Since ​mid-September 2026, a further extension to the original ​Yongbyon enrichment facility has been under construction with the new extension externally complete by ​late October 2026," Grossi said, with no further details.

He said the ongoing expansion of North Korea's uranium enrichment program remains "a cause for serious concern."

Enriched uranium is used as fuel for nuclear power plants and the nuclear reactors that run naval ships and submarines. It can also be used in nuclear weapons.

US President Donald Trump floated the possibility of a meeting with North Korean leader Kim Jong Un as he went to China to meet with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin.

Meanwhile, North Korean state media — which typically operates behind the global news cycle — has only released images of Kim visiting a ski resort and Mount Paektu, making no mention of possible Shenzhen travel plans.

Kim has said he aims to arm the nation with an expansive nuclear force to counter the US and South Korea.

In March, soon after the US and Israel launched strikes on Iran, Kim accused Washington of "global terrorism and aggression."

The Iran war reinforced North Korea's long-standing view that it needs a nuclear arsenal to be able to defend itself against what it sees as its two largest enemies, South Korea and the US.

North Korea has been subject to various international sanctions over its nuclear weapons development and use of ballistic missile technology. But Pyongyang has repeatedly flouted the restrictions.

In September 2024, North Korea revealed its first covert uranium-enrichment plant. Kim delivered a speech then, including a call for "exponentially" expanding the country's nuclear arsenal.

In April, Grossi told reporters that his agency had confirmed "a rapid increase” in activities at nuclear facilities in North Korea.

In September, the IAEA passed resolutions at its annual conference regarding concerns about the North Korean nuclear program.

Kim Yo Jong, the powerful sister of Kim Jong Un, said in response that the country's nuclear-armed status was "absolute."

She called those expecting denuclearisation "idiots."

#212. Waymo pulls driverless taxis from freeways across the US after California ban

The autonomous driving company suspended driverless taxi rides on US highways days after California banned the vehicles on its own freeways

Waymo has stopped offering driverless taxi rides on freeways across the US indefinitely. The Alphabet subsidiary pulled its vehicles from highways nationwide after California banned them from freeways in response to recent traffic incidents.

Waymo had allowed riders to hail its robotaxis for freeway rides in the San Francisco Bay Area, Los Angeles, Phoenix, Miami and Austin. The California-based company offers rides on city streets in many other areas, including Las Vegas, Dallas and Denver. Those rides remain unaffected by suspended freeway service.

A Waymo spokesperson wrote in an email that the company has “temporarily restricted commercial freeway rides in all markets where we operate that service”, though it’s “continuing freeway testing with trained autonomous specialists behind the wheel”. The suspension was implemented last Wednesday, according to the spokesperson, but they didn’t say how long it would last.

Waymo’s voluntary suspension came the same day as the California department of motor vehicles banned the company’s robotaxis from the state’s freeways. The ban followed two traffic incidents, including one in which three Waymo driverless vehicles drove into a construction zone, a DMV spokesperson, Jonathan Groveman, said in a statement.

The construction zone incident occurred on 5 November, and the second incident, which involved California highway patrol vehicles, occurred the next day on a highway in San Francisco, according to Groveman.

Video of the 6 November incident circulated widely on social media last week. In the footage, a Waymo vehicle can be seen trying to bypass California highway patrol vehicles conducting a traffic break.

“The department determined that Waymo’s driverless freeway operations pose an imminent hazard to public safety due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present,” Groveman said.

Groveman added that “similar incidents” previously occurred on 18 May.

A weekly dive in to how technology is shaping our lives

The Waymo spokesperson said the “recent freeway construction zone encounters on US 101 in California” did not “involve any injuries or collisions”.

California’s restrictions bar Waymo from operating and testing its driverless vehicles on the state’s freeways, according to Groveman. He said the restrictions will remain in effect until “Waymo demonstrates to the department that it has taken effective corrective actions to address the deficiencies that precipitated the restrictions”.

#211. Amidst fears of AI cyberattacks, Satya Nadella says lots of critical infrastructure is ‘unpatchable’

During his appearance at The New York Times’ DealBook Summit on Wednesday, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella lamented the fact that a lot of America’s software systems — including those governing everyday hardware and transportation systems — suffer from unfixable software vulnerabilities that, in the age of AI-aided cyberattacks, are just waiting to be exploited.

“Remember, there is a lot of stuff in our critical infrastructure that’s unpatchable,” Nadella said. “I mean, think about all the firmware and the chillers [a cooling device often used in data centers] and the HVAC systems everywhere else. So all of that, by the way, they’re on the internet.”

In other words: The smartification of the economy, in which much of the world’s “dumb” devices were hooked up to the internet, is now causing tremendous headaches.

The conversation took place against the backdrop of a wave of recent large-scale outages — including one involving Microsoft Outlook. Others have involved the NSA, the FFA, and Apple. Concerns have swirled that these outages are the result of cyberattacks, although officials have denied those claims. Still, the reality of AI-aided cyberattacks is spurring companies to find new strategies for vulnerability discovery and remediation.

Nadella touched briefly on the recent Outlook situation, noting that the global inconvenience was the result of a “latent bug” that led to a “perfect storm.”

“We put out an incident response, and we’re going to obviously take all of the learnings, both upstream architectural, as well as operational, and fix that,” he added.

“Somebody was telling me about sort of even the transportation systems and the amount of stuff that’s discoverable but with unpatchable firmware,” Nadella also added on the topic of vulnerable systems. “So we’ll have to really modernize for sure … and then we will have to assess what the threat vector looks like, what our own ability to both detect and then defend in real time looks like.”

Nadella made a broader point as well, which is that the process of patching software vulnerabilities has escalated to match the speed of potential exploitation, but that this has caused more problems.

“We now have a very powerful tool that finds vulnerabilities,” he said. However, companies used to have more time to test those patches, understand a situation, and deploy them, he said. At the same time, AI has made sophisticated cyberattacks much easier to enact.

“The patch [now] needs to go instantly from being available to being patched in production, because it’s being exploited in the wild, right? So that’s going to create instability.”

#210. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

China Renaissance said in late February that it had learned Bao was “cooperating in an investigation” being carried out by certain authorities in the country. It gave no other details.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#208. Elon Musk says: ‘I’m not MAGA’

Elon Musk, who spent $277 million to get Donald Trump reelected president in 2024 and who, during that campaign, declared himself “dark MAGA” and who, after the election, was subsequently photographed sporting a cartoonishly large MAGA hat while visiting the Oval Office, has some news for Americans: None of that necessarily makes him MAGA.

“I’m not MAGA,” Musk declared Wednesday during a conversation with Andrew Ross Sorkin at The New York Times DealBook Summit. Musk joined the conversation via video call, as he is currently in China, joining President Trump for the APEC summit.

“Well, at least I’m not like … I’m not sure what MAGA means,” he backtracked. “If that means sort of blind adherence to a set of policies by the Republican Party, then I certainly don’t have that.”

The comments were made amidst a broader question about how Musk felt in the wake of this year’s midterms, during which a “blue wave” led to Democratic victories throughout the country. Musk, notably, spent a gargantuan amount of money (reportedly some $445 million) to support Republican candidates. Musk admitted Wednesday that such vast political spending might not have been a good use of his money.

“I feel pretty foolish about having spent so much money and not seen much for it,” he said. “Or maybe it would have been much worse without that. But it certainly shows that, you know, American elections are not just about money.”

During his conversation with Sorkin, Musk also seemed to lean away from his viral edgelord persona and claimed that he held no extreme views. He said: “I don’t think I have like, you know, any kind of extreme political views.”

Sorkin pushed Musk on this a little, noting that some people found his views on immigration extreme, to which Musk said: “Like what?” Mild laughter spread throughout the audience.

Ironically, the last time Musk spoke at DealBook, in 2023, he was in the middle of a very public battle with his social media site X’s advertisers. Critics had accused Musk of platforming a typhoon of hateful content on his site, and advertisers were threatening to walk over it. In response, Musk famously told Sorkin that he hoped those advertisers would “go f—” themselves.

Now Musk has an entirely new set of concerns. Democratic lawmakers in various committees have signaled that they may investigate Musk and his business activities as they probe potential corruption throughout the Trump administration.

Specifically, officials have said they’re interested in scrutinizing Musk’s role in DOGE, the “government efficiency” initiative that Musk launched inside the White House at the start of Trump’s new term, as well as Starlink’s activities in Ukraine, where the company has been accused of throttling the military’s access to its internet service.

Sorkin broached some of the potential concerns for the Tesla CEO: “You hear people like the chairman of the oversight committee saying, ‘Lawyer up,’ and you hear the president saying, ‘If I have to give him a pardon’ — meaning you — ‘I’ll give him a pardon.’”

Musk replied: “I don’t think I need a pardon by the way, because I haven’t committed any crimes.”

Musk then made one of those controversial statements he’s known for, appearing to compare an incoming Texas official to Lavrentiy Beria, the top head of the Soviet secret police under Josef Stalin.

“The incoming Texas, you know, House Majority Leader, said that I should go to prison,” Musk said. “I’m like, the guy’s trying to, you know channel Beria from, like, Stalin, where it’s, you know, show me the man, I’ll show you the crime type of thing.”

It’s not clear who (or what) Musk was talking about. Texas doesn’t have a House Majority Leader. However, he may have been referencing Gene Wu, who is the incoming chair of the Texas House Democratic caucus and who has suggested that Musk be investigated for potential criminal activity.

“Hopefully, in this country, you still need to do an actual crime before you go to prison,” Musk added.

#207. Record-breaking rain lashes California’s Bay area as ‘atmospheric river’ arrives

Storm expected to move into southern California, with LA residents warned of potential evacuation orders

Heavy rain pounded California’s Bay area, with record rainfall in downtown San Francisco and flooding and power outages across the region, as an “atmospheric river” arrived on Wednesday and dumped precipitation across the state.

The storm was expected to move into southern California on Wednesday night and Thursday, with Los Angeles warning residents in mudslide-prone areas of potential evacuation orders to come. Southern Californians were also warned of potential coastal flooding, heavy surf, wave damage to coastal piers and parking lots and beach sand erosion. Heavy snow warnings were issued for higher elevations of the Sierra Mountains, including in the Greater Lake Tahoe area.

Downtown San Francisco saw the most rainfall before noon in 76 years, at 2.11in, surpassing a record from 1950, the San Francisco Chronicle reported. By 3pm, Wednesday’s daily total of 2.28in had broken the record for the most rainfall on 18 November, which was previously set in 1918, the National Weather Service reported. South of San Francisco, in Santa Clara, there was flooding and standing water on several highways.

In San Jose, the local fire department posted on social media that two vehicles with passengers inside had become stranded in the flooded streets downtown near the SAP Center, the city’s major events arena. The second passenger was being extricated via a boat rescue, the department wrote.

Daniel Lurie, the San Francisco mayor, warned city residents the region’s winter storm and flood season, supercharged by this year’s very strong El Niño weather, was just getting started.

Despite the Wednesday afternoon break in the rain, he posted on social media, “The storm should pick back up tonight, and more rain is in the forecast for next week.”

“When these heavy storms come, we expect flooding to return to many of the same areas that were impacted this morning,” he added.

“Today’s storm certainly gave the Bay Area a good assessment of our pain points for future storms this season,” Drew Tuma, a local meteorologist for ABC7 News, wrote on social media.

Street flooding in San Francisco left maintenance hole covers floating in one neighborhood of San Francisco, while the self-driving car company Waymo temporarily suspended service in the city, the Chronicle reported. One Waymo stalled in a flooded street and blocked traffic, ABC7 News reported.

An overwhelmed sewer stormwater system also appeared to result in several “sewage geysers”, one reportedly 15-ft tall, the Chronicle reported, sharing bystander footage of the spew in the city’s Ocean Beach neighborhood.

Thousands across the Bay area were dealing with storm-related power outages by Wednesday afternoon, according to a customer outage map from Pacific Gas & Electric, the major utility company for much of northern California.

In Monterey, south of San Francisco, the local aquarium posted on social media that “storms and massive waves are causing a surge of sea otter strandings this week in Monterey Bay” and that their staff “safely rescued several pups” after receiving calls from local community members.

Areas of the state still recovering from recent wildfires continue to be at risk for flash flooding, with specific warnings issued for “burn scar” areas including in Big Sur and the Napa valley.

In Los Angeles, city officials warned residents of areas burned by the 2025 Palisades, Sunset and Hurst fires, north of the city, to be prepared for potential evacuation orders starting on Wednesday night, as heavy rains brought increased risk of flooding, mudslide and debris flow.

Statewide, the heaviest of the rainfall was expected north-east of San Francisco, from Santa Rosa to Chico and Redding in the state’s Central valley, and in the southern part of the Bay Area, along coastal mountain ranges from Santa Cruz to San Jose and Palo Alto, according to the National Weather Service.

“Travel delays, localized flooding and hazardous conditions are possible, especially along the coast and on mountain pass roads,” AccuWeather warned in an online forecast.

Atmospheric rivers, massive currents of water vapor that can carry as much or even more water than an actual river, have been growing stronger and deadlier in recent years. In the relatively dry western US, atmospheric river storms provide both essential precipitation and flood risk.

In southern California, the NWS predicted 1-3in of rain in many areas, with 3in-5in in some mountain and foothill areas, and a 20% chance of thunderstorms on Wednesday and Thursday. As much as a foot of snow was expected in higher-elevation areas above 6,000ft. There was a high risk of “significant road flooding” and a moderate risk for rockslides and mudslides, the NWS said.

The risk of coastal wave damage was expected to peak in southern California on Thursday, with additional risks expected next week, with seasonal high tides overlapping with storm risks during Thanksgiving week.

Waterspouts were spotted over the San Francisco Bay earlier this week, and the NWS flagged the risk of additional waterspouts over the ocean east of San Francisco on Wednesday.

#206. Borussia Dortmund bows to fan pressure and end Rheinmetall deal

Borussia Dortmund has reluctantly ended its association with a major sponsor after long-term fan protests. They are not the first Bundesliga club to u-turn, as commercial interests clash with politics and fan culture.

Two years of fan pressure and criticism has forced Borussia Dortmund to drop a sponsorship deal with Germany's largest arms manufacturer, Rheinmetall.

The Bundesliga club will not renew its deal with Rheinmetall beyond its expiry in June 2027, at the end of this season. Dortmund chairman Carsten Cramer confirmed the news on the club's website ahead of its AGM on Sunday, which may have proved a potential flashpoint. He acknowledged that "our partnership has been controversial from the outset" but lamented the loss of income.

"It reduces our financial room for manoeuvre. We will have to reprioritize planned investments and carefully examine how we can find viable solutions."

Though he welcomed the news, one of the leaders of the fan revolt against the partnership, Michael Schulze von Glaser, warned this is likely not the end of the road.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"I think in the end it was a victory for the fans. But I'm also a bit skeptical about  what the future will bring us," the Dortmund fan and former head of the German Peace Society told DW. "Football fans in Germany always must be skeptical and always must watch what the club is doing.”

Many Dortmund fans have opposed the deal — which has seen Rheinmetall's logo on advertising boards and other surfaces at locations such as Dortmund's 83,000-capacity Westfalenstadion — from its beginnings in 2024. It was estimated to be worth between €7 ($7.68 million) and €9 million per year.

At the club's AGM that year, 556 of 855 members present voted against the deal in a non-binding vote. There were 247 votes in favour, with 52 abstentions. This, said Cramer, informed the eventual outcome.

Rheinmetall sells arms mostly to Germany, Ukraine and NATO but fans and critics feel that platforming any arms manufacturer goes against the club's statement of its core values. In that, Dortmund states its desire for "a club and a society that operate free of racism, anti-Semitism, LGBTQIA+ hostility, sexism, violence and discrimination."

To play this audio please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 audio

It has always been clear, said Schulze von Glaser, that this deal did not chime with that.

"In the end, they kill people. They are for war," he said of Rheinmetall. "The slogan of Borussia Dortmund is true love (Echte Liebe). But for me and many others, our love ends there…. I know some fans who have put fan merchandise in the trash because they were so shocked.”

In the Q and A on the club's website, Cramer said he was still a "personal advocate" of the deal but that Dortmund's commitment to the 50+1 model, which means German clubs are majority controlled by the members, meant they had to listen to fan voices. But he also warned that Rheinmetall must be an "absolute exception and an isolated case."

That set off some alarm bells for Schulze von Glaser.

"It's a bit concerning that there are constantly these attacks on professional football — a new sponsorship, investment deals," he said.

Indeed, Dortmund's climbdown is not the first of its kind in Germany's top flight. Last year, after fan and media pressure, Bayern Munich ended its commercial deal with the tourism arm of the Rwandan government before its conclusion.

Fans had objected to the promotion of Rwanda, which many felt sought to divert attention from its poor human rights record and actions in the war with DR Congo, through sporting partnerships. Two years earlier, Bayern withdrew from a similarly controversial deal with Qatar Airways.

Elsewhere in the league, Werder Bremen fans objected to their long time shirt sponsor, Wiesenhof. Many supporters took issue with the factory farming practices  of Germany's largest poultry producer. Though it was not given as a direct reason for the end of a shirt sponsorship deal in 2023, it likely played a part.

Union Berlin fans have also made their voices heard. When the club signed a new shirt-sponsorship deal with Luxembourg real estate company, Aroundtown, upon promotion to the top flight in 2019, many fans were frustrated at the tie up in a city where rent prices were skyrocketing.

"It's a problem for a lot of people," fan Sven Dobberstein told DW at the time.

"We have to try to find a way between commercialization and what we have at the moment. It isn't easy."

Striking that balance can often be a source of tension for Bundesliga clubs. 50+1 ensures fans have a voice, preventing rich owners, or nation states, from taking control as they do in England's lucrative Premier League or in France or Spain. Indeed fan protests have also stopped the league signing a €1billion private equity partner and seen Monday night matches scrapped. For Schulze von Glaser, this needs to be protected.

"In Germany, the fan culture has an impact," he said.

”If there is something bad or terrible happening in your club and in the Bundesliga, there are the organized fans and they can have an impact if they want, if they stand together and, yes, they can stop the Rheinmetall deal."

#204. Elon Musk claims ‘I’m not Maga’ and says he regrets vast sums to back Republicans

Musk, who aligned with Trump and was frequent White House guest, says he feels ‘pretty foolish’ about record midterm spending

Elon Musk denied being Maga on Wednesday, despite aligning himself intensely with Donald Trump’s Make America Great Again movement since 2024.

“I’m not Maga,” the world’s richest person told the New York Times Dealbook summit, which he attended via video call from China, where he is one of several US business leaders who have accompanied Trump on his trip to Asia.

“I’m not sure what Maga means, but if that means sort of blind adherence to a set of policies by the Republican party, then I certainly don’t have that,” the chief executive of SpaceX and Tesla added.

Musk famously declared at a 2024 campaign rally in Madison Square Garden: “I’m not just Maga. I’m dark, gothic Maga.”

In a 45-minute interview with Andrew Ross Sorkin, a New York Times columnist, Musk also said it would be good for Washington to have split control between the two parties, and that he thinks “there are positives and negatives in both”.

He also accused Ukraine’s president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, of “lying” about restrictions on access to his firm’s Starshield satellite system; praised Jensen Huang, the ousted Nvidia chief executive; and said that “no one I met at Anthropic set off my evil detector”.

Asked about his previous negative comments about Anthropic, Musk said that before he agreed to rent computing power to the AI firm: “I went to met with a lot of people at Anthropic. I didn’t just move forward with the deal.”

“My interaction so far with the Anthropic team is that they seem like good people and they seem principled,” Musk added. “They seem diligent and they seem seem to care about the future.”

Musk donated at least $250m of his own money to help Trump win the election in 2024, and recently spent nearly $500m in the US midterms to support Republicans, many of whom lost.

Musk admitted at the Dealbook summit that his spending had “failed”, and that he felt “pretty foolish” for spending such a sum, the most that anyone has ever spent in a political cycle when they themselves did not run for office. “It certainly shows that American elections are not just about money,” he said. “If I could rewind the clock, I would have spent less money.”

After Trump took office for the second time in 2025, Musk served for 180 days heading the so-called “department of government efficiency” (Doge) within Trump’s administration, before stepping down following a very public falling out with the president.

That culminated in Musk posting on his social media platform in June of that year: “Time to drop the really big bomb: @realDonaldTrump is in the Epstein files. That is the real reason they have not been made public.”

The two men later reconciled. During his time leading Doge, Musk frequently appeared in the Oval Office and at cabinet meetings alongside the president, often sporting hats that read “Make America Great Again” or “Trump was right about everything”.

#203. Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella explains why he asked 60K employees to quit

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella explained Wednesday why he offered buyouts to 60,000 of his employees in October, essentially urging them to voluntarily quit instead of reducing the workforce via another massive layoff.

Short answer: AI, but not, he insists, in the way you might expect. He’s not saying he’s replacing employees with AI, especially software engineers. He’s saying he’s reorganizing Microsoft into a company where everyone uses AI to do their work, and their jobs become focused on managing that AI.

The buyout was “to make sure that at least everyone felt like they had agency in making choices,” he told Andrew Ross Sorkin during the DealBook Summit.

“Let’s take software engineering. We went from, ‘Well, we don’t buy that AI can ever do software.’ To suddenly, ‘Wow, it can do all software, and why do we need software engineers?’ To now rediscovering that software engineering is very much alive and, in fact, is going to thrive, except the way the team is organized and the work that a software engineer does has now got a new operating point,” he explained.

For some people, “that reorganization and change is sort of hard, right?” he added, “but, I mean, it has to be done.”

He offered the example of the team that builds Cowork, the AI agent in Microsoft 365 apps. He said the team consists of just 11 developers who have mastered using AI to do their jobs. “They build a software factory that builds the product. They don’t build the product.”

He believes that every employee will one day similarly become a manager of agents, rather than a person directly doing the work. “That level of direction is what, by the way, is going to happen in all jobs.”

But Microsoft, as a 51-year-old company, didn’t grow up as an AI-first company. “So the question is, how quickly can an incumbent transform the work, the workflow, and the work artifact, and with all the wage support that goes with it?” he said. “It’s going to take a bit more time than anyone expects. And there is going to be in all of this, you know, people who may say, ‘Hey, I have some other choices I want to make.’”

When asked about this same topic — why ask 60,000 people to voluntarily resign — last week by Gergely Orosz on his The Pragmatic Engineer podcast, Nadella further explained, “We have created enormous anxiety in people sort of feeling that they don’t have control of their own destiny because the way we have talked about this AI revolution.”

He called out talk about the singularity (the hypothetical point at which AI surpasses human intelligence) and predictions that AI will take away all jobs. “All of that leads anyone who even wants to change to be sort of anxious … You have to sort of walk the hard walk of really making the structural changes, getting people slotted, giving them opportunity.”

In other words, the buyout was the message to employees: You can get with the new AI-first program, which will radically change your job, or you choose to get on with your life. Elsewhere.

#201. US House Democrats nominate Hakeem Jeffries for speaker

Jeffries is set to be the first Black speaker of the US House of Representatives when Democrats take control of the chamber next year. He will be second in line to the presidency.

Democrats in the US House of Representatives on Wednesday nominated Hakeem Jeffries to be the next speaker.

Jeffries, who represents a district in New York, would be the first Black person to fill the role. He received 251 out of 265 ballots cast in a closed-door, secret vote.

"Thank you House Democrats. Together, we will deliver bold change," Jeffries posted on X after the vote. "And make a big difference for the American people!"

Some progressive Democrats voted against Jeffries' speakership, with the congressman considered by some to be in the moderate wing of the party.

The entire House will do a roll call vote on January 3, 2027, to elect a speaker when the new Congress convenes. As Democrats won control of the House of Representatives in the midterm elections, claiming at least 257 of 435 seats in the lower chamber of US Congress, the 56-year-old Jeffries is all but certain to get the position.

In a rebuke of US President Donald Trump's Republican Party, Democrats also won 56 seats and control of the Senate, the upper house of US Congress.

Jeffries will steer Democratic priorities in the House, such as possible legislation and investigations of the Trump administration. He will also be second in line to the presidency after Vice President JD Vance.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Following the vote, seven of the 11 Democrats who voted against Jeffries issued a joint statement.

"Whether on Medicare for All, stopping ICE terror, getting money out of politics, or ending the genocide in Gaza, our constituents sent us to Congress with a mandate on these issues," Representatives-elect Darializa Avila Chevalier, Adam Hamawy, Frederick Haynes, Melat Kiros, Donavan McKinney, Chris Rabb and Claire Valdez wrote.

"We have worked to secure commitments from Leader Jeffries in good-faith conversations. While we have seen meaningful progress, there is still work to be done to ensure that the direction our party takes this coming Congress reflects what our constituents are demanding of leadership," they added. "Those conversations are ongoing. As such, our vote in caucus today was no."

Under Jeffries, Massachusetts Representative Katherine Clark will serve as female majority leader, and Pete Aguilar of California will serve as majority whip. Clark will be the first woman to serve in that position, with Aguilar the first Latino to fill his elected role in the House.

For the Republican House delegation in the new Congress, Jim Jordan, a representative of Ohio, will be the next House minority leader. Jordan is a staunch Trump supporter.

In the Senate, Chuck Schumer of New York is the frontrunner to lead Democrats in the upper chamber, with Maryland Senator Chris Van Hollen also in the running.

Republican South Dakota Senator John Thune, who currently serves as Senate majority leader, is expected to be minority leader next year.

“They’re all individual events. These are big companies. They’re putting in new equipment and things happen when you put in new equipment. And these are all individual things,” he said.
