#163. World’s top court to rule if Myanmar committed genocide against Rohingya

International court of justice has never before found a state guilty of genocide, and ruling will have implications for case against Israel

The world’s top court will deliver a judgment this week on whether Myanmar committed genocide against its Rohingya minority, in a landmark case that will set a critical 21st-century benchmark for the gravest of all international crimes.

The closely watched judgment could become one of the most significant moments in international law since the end of the second world war.

The international court of justice (ICJ) has never found a state responsible for genocide, a crime defined after the Nazi-led Holocaust. The ruling will have implications for a separate high-profile genocide case against Israel.

Nearly a decade after a military campaign forced hundreds of thousands of Muslim Rohingya to flee across the border, the ICJ in The Hague will deliver its judgment on Thursday morning.

“The ICJ ruling will be historic for Rohingya survivors and their families around the world,” said Yanghee Lee, a former UN rights investigator to Myanmar.

“The international community failed to prevent Myanmar military atrocities against the Rohingya nine years ago. It must seize this opportunity to finally secure justice for the Rohingya and begin to reckon with these failings.”

ICJ judges have spent seven years weighing accusations by the Gambia, a Muslim-majority state, that Myanmar’s repression of the Rohingya breached the 1948 UN genocide convention.

A key issue in previous cases has been how to prove “intent” in a crime defined as acts committed with the “intent to destroy, in whole or in part” a national, ethnical, racial or religious group.

Antonia Mulvey, the founder of Legal Action Worldwide (Law), a group that represents hundreds of Rohingya survivors, said she was “cautiously optimistic” the court would hold Myanmar fully accountable.

Government actions showed “patterns of conduct” that proved clear attempts to destroy the group, she said.

“The attacks on the villages took place the same way,” she said. “They would attack the civilians, burn the villages, separate men and women, conduct sexual violence … and then as they fled, they would continue to kill them.”

Two other ICJ cases dealing with atrocities in the Balkans fell short of finding states directly responsible for genocide, although Serbia was found legally responsible for failing to prevent the genocide at Srebrenica.

Mulvey said a key part of the upcoming judgment will be how it handles the issue of rape and sexual violence as an act of genocide. Her team has collected accounts of soldiers mutilating women’s genitals and biting their faces. “That’s to mark people, to brand them … it is also to stop reproduction,” she said.

“Yes, we would say sexual violence is a critical part to show their intent to destroy the group,” she added.

“You don’t need to gang-rape people. You don’t need to kill their children in front of them. You don’t need to mutilate them. You don’t need to do it in public. What is the purpose of that? The purpose is to destroy the group.”

In January, the ICJ held closed-door sessions to hear directly from Rohingya survivors who travelled from camps in Bangladesh. One witness said her child was taken from her arms, stabbed and thrown into a fire, according to Law, while another described seeing piles of bodies in a mass grave.

During hearings, Gambian justice minister Dawda Jallow told the court the case was not “about esoteric issues of international law”.

“It is about real people, real stories and a real group of human beings,” said Jallow. “The Rohingya of Myanmar. They have been targeted for destruction.”

Myanmar, which has denied carrying out a genocide, claims its actions targeted a military uprising from insurgents.

For decades, Rohingya people have been stripped of their citizenship and targeted by increasing discrimination, amid a backdrop of communal violence with Buddhists in the country’s western Rakhine state.

The crisis reached a climax after a Rohingya militant group conducted guerrilla-style attacks in the summer of 2017. The Myanmar army responded with massive “clearance operations”.

Refugees who fled into Bangladesh in 2017 told the Guardian of a massacre in the village of Tula Toli, where survivors described systematic shooting of civilians, including the drowning of children in a river.

This testimony from Tula Toli – which satellite footage later showed had been razed – added to dozens of witness accounts collected over the years to build a case for genocide.

A ruling that Myanmar committed genocide would add international pressure on its leader Min Aung Hlaing, who headed the military during the 2016-2017 attacks and later overthrew the elected government of Aung San Suu Kyi in a 2021 coup, triggering a civil war that has left tens of thousands dead.

The former general is separately b​eing investigated by the international criminal court (ICC), which prosecutes individuals and not states like the ICJ, on allegations of crimes against humanity. He was installed as civilian president in April after elections that were widely condemned as a sham, and has made efforts to rebuild diplomatic ties.

For many onlookers, the ICJ decision will hold relevance far beyond Myanmar, as it will provide the strongest hint of how judges might assess the seminal genocide case brought by South Africa against Israel over its campaign in Gaza.

The cases have much in common, with an ethnic group targeted by a powerful military, although Israel has far stronger diplomatic backing from the US.

The judgment comes as Washington has been on the offensive against international human rights bodies, including sanctioning the ICC. Washington technically already considers the Rohingya crisis to be a genocide, based on a decision by the Biden administration.

However, Trump’s UN ambassador, Mike Waltz, warned that the ICJ “must not be allowed” to establish a standard for genocide that could be used against “allied soldiers”, a statement that will be relevant for Israeli troops.

Supporters of the Myanmar case say the judgment should compel states to block arms transfers and jet fuel supplies. While the court does not have the means to enforce its decisions, they have been respected in the past, and the UN security council could give it strength, although divisions, especially between the US and China, make this unlikely.

More than 1 million Rohingya remain in Bangladesh, in the world’s largest refugee settlement. Fewer than 600,000 are estimated to remain in Myanmar.

A displaced Rohingya living inside Myanmar told the Guardian he hoped the judgment would “help end decades of persecution and discrimination”.

“We are not asking for sympathy alone,” he said. “We are asking for justice, recognition, equal rights and a future in which our children can live safely and with dignity in their own homeland.”

→ Baca artikel asli

#162. Would Ukraine trade land for NATO membership or protection?

The US is exploring whether Ukraine would cede parts of Donetsk province still under its control in return for NATO membership or protection. Experts warn the idea is risky.

The US has asked Ukraine whether it would be willing to cede Donetsk province to Russia in return for NATO membership. Together, Donetsk and Luhansk constitute the so-called Donbas region. Russia currently controls most of Luhansk province, and large swathes of Donetsk, though Ukraine continues holding a number of major cities there, including Kramatorsk and Sloviansk.

So far, no concrete proposal had been put forward, according to Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy. He said he would publicly discuss the matter once the US and NATO member states had agreed on the plan, stressing that he could not make any such decision "on his own." Zelenskyy also said any potential agreement would go ahead "without recognizing Russia's occupation of these territories."

The Ukrainian leader said the US was talking about guarantees similar to those enshrined in Articles 4 and 5 of the North Atlantic Treaty, though did not envision US troops on Ukrainian soil. Article 4 provides for consultations among member states in the event of imminent threats, while Article 5 stipulates that an attack on one state is considered an attack on all. The US, however, has said it pictures bilateral guarantees for Ukraine similar to those established with Israel and certain Middle Eastern countries.

Russia has yet to comment on these proposals, with President Putin's spokesperson Dmitry Peskov stating that "we maintain our position that, in the interest of the peace process, it is not appropriate to disclose details of the talks between us and the Americans."

Experts interviewed by DW are skeptical of the land-for-security-guarantees idea, objecting that the US cannot uphold such an arrangement on its own.

"It seems to me that this is what happens to normal diplomacy when it's been run by real estate agents who are used to selling people something that doesn't really exist," Oleksandr Kraiev of the North America-Israel Research Program at Ukraine's Ukrainian Prism think tank tells DW. He says that at least half of NATO's member states have reservations about Ukraine's accession.

"We'll just end up with a second Budapest Memorandum, it'll just carry a different name," Kraiev told DW. The original 1994 Budapest Memorandum stipulated that Russia, the US and UK respect Ukraine's independence and sovereignty in its existing borders and refrain from the threat or use of force to change its territorial integrity. Ukraine in return committed to giving up all nuclear weapons.

The Budapest Memorandum, however, did not contain any commitment by the UK or US to defend Ukraine in the event of an attack.

Most experts interviewed by DW are critical of the idea of holding a referendum to let ordinary Ukrainians decide whether their country should hand over full control of Donetsk province in exchange for NATO membership.

This is because ceding territory would not only violate Ukraine's constitution and harm its national interests, but would also undermine a world order "in which borders cannot be redrawn by force," Oleksandr Khara of Ukraine's Centre for Defence Strategies told DW. "It doesn't matter whether the citizens of Ukraine vote for or against such a proposal," Khara said, adding that any such referendum would set "a very dangerous precedent."

A survey by the Kyiv International Institute of Sociology (KISS) conducted between October 8 and 16 found that 61% of respondents categorically reject withdrawing troops from Ukrainian-controlled parts of the Donbas, such as cities like Kramatorsk and Sloviansk, even in exchange for US and European security guarantees. 35% of respondents said they would begrudgingly accept a land-for-security deal.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Defense analyst Khara doubts a bilateral agreement between Ukraine and the US, akin to those established between the US and Israel, would guarantee Kyiv any more support than it presently receives.

"This concerns financial support, joint weapons production and development, and the training of Ukrainian troops — everything that falls below the threshold of collective security," Khara told DW. "It is good that these commitments — security agreements, political declarations, and the allocation of funds in the budgets of the countries supporting us — are set out in writing, at least for the next five years." Yet to prevent another war, Khara believes that ideally, Ukraine needs "a nuclear shield," specifically, a "strategic one from the US" and a "tactical one from Europe."

Ukrainian experts already floated to the idea of an alternative security arrangement to NATO members in December 2023, says Sergiy Solodkyy of Ukraine's New Europe research center. The arrangement would see Ukraine join the alliance within its internationally recognized borders, though specific protocols would stipulate that only territories under Kyiv's control would be defended.

This arrangement could be modeled on the German case, where West Germany became a member of NATO first, while East Germany joined the alliance only after German reunification.

West Germany never gave up on reunification, Solodkyy told DW. "That means we, too, should not give up on our territory or recognize the border." That is why he says the land-for-security deal proposed to Ukraine is unacceptable.

"This is a trap that Russia and the US negotiators are trying to lure the Ukrainian side into," Solodkyy told DW.

#161. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.

#158. Can a US-backed mineral highway bring peace and prosperity to DR Congo?

The Lobito Corridor links DR Congo's mineral-rich south to Angola's Atlantic coast through the Benguela Railway and the Port of Lobito

As trains carrying thousands of tonnes of copper from the Democratic Republic of Congo roar through her community, 20-year-old Manuela Julia is not thinking about critical minerals or billion-dollar investments.

She is thinking about getting around more easily.

The railway runs through Catumbela, near the Angolan city of Lobito, on the Atlantic coast.

For Julia, it has made travelling cheaper and more convenient.

"Before, I had to use a taxi, which is much more expensive. Now it is cheaper and easier to travel," she says.

But the railway has ambitions far bigger than improving local transport.

It is part of the Lobito Corridor, a US-backed effort to secure access to Africa's critical minerals and promote regional economic cooperation, which Washington hopes will help bring peace to conflict-torn eastern DR Congo.

Under agreements brokered by US President Donald Trump, DR Congo and neighbouring Rwanda have committed to greater economic cooperation, including more transparent mineral supply chains and regional infrastructure development.

The wider plan envisages linking these initiatives to the railway, which transports minerals from Central Africa to Western markets via Angola, rather than east to China as most do currently.

The hope is that formalising the mineral trade and encouraging investment could help curb illicit markets that have helped finance armed groups and fuel instability.

Manuela Julia says the railway has made travelling around easier

But can a railway built to move minerals to global markets also deliver peace and prosperity?

The Lobito Corridor links DR Congo's mineral-rich south to Angola's Atlantic coast through the Benguela Railway and the Port of Lobito.

Its importance has grown as demand for copper and cobalt rises, driven partly by the global transition to cleaner energy, as both are critical ingredients for all kinds of electrical components.

"The timing matters," says Johannesburg-based political risk analyst Wellington Muzengeza

"Electrification and the energy transition have increased the strategic importance of copper, cobalt and reliable mineral supply chains."

DR Congo is the world's largest producer of cobalt and one of its biggest copper producers, along with neighbouring Zambia. The US sees the corridor as a means to diversify mineral supplies and reduce reliance on China, which dominates much of the world's processing capacity.

A US-DR Congo strategic partnership identifies the route from the Congolese town of Sakania to Lobito as a priority for mineral exports, while the economic framework agreed by DR Congo and Rwanda envisages connecting regional infrastructure and trade initiatives to the corridor.

The project has attracted over $750m (Â£570m), including funding backed by the US International Development Finance Corporation and the Development Bank of Southern Africa to upgrade railway tracks, signalling systems and trains.

The railway, built in 1902 under Portuguese colonial rule, was originally designed to transport goods and minerals from Central Africa to the Atlantic coast. After years of disruption during Angola's civil war, it was rebuilt and fully reopened in 2015.

The US first got involved in the project under former President Joe Biden but backing has continued under Trump.

At Lobito's mineral terminal, the renewed ambitions are already visible.

When the BBC visited, a train carrying about 12,000 tonnes of copper cathodes from DR Congo had arrived. Workers moved the refined copper sheets into a warehouse before they were loaded on to ships bound for international markets, mostly the US.

The cargo is handled by Lobito Atlantic Railway (LAR), which has a 30-year concession to operate freight services along the corridor and manage the mineral terminal.

The Lobito Atlantic Railway has a 30-year concession to operate freight services along the corridor

Chief operating officer Nicholas Gregoir says freight volumes have more than doubled since the company took over in January 2024.

LAR expects to transport about 200,000 tonnes of copper through the corridor in 2026.

For mining companies, the railway offers a shorter route to international markets.

"The Lobito Corridor is the shortest route for both Zambia and the DRC," to the Atlantic, says KÃ¡tila Vanessa Monteiro da Silva Neto, executive director of the Lobito Port Authority.

Zambia, Africa's second-largest copper producer, also plans to connect its Copperbelt to Lobito through a proposed 830km (515 mile)-railway.

But a more efficient route to market does not automatically translate into economic transformation.

"Joining a corridor is not the same as industrialising," Muzengeza says. "The test is not simply whether Zambia gains another route to the sea. It is whether that route helps build a stronger economy."

Some Angolans believe the railway's greatest value could lie in what it offers businesses and communities beyond mining.

"I call it a corridor of development," says businessman AdÃ©rito Areias.

The railway passes through some of Angola's most productive agricultural regions.

Better transport links could help farmers reach larger markets and reduce losses.

"If there isn't an artery carrying products from the interior to where there is demand, those products simply rot where they are produced," Areias says.

But the challenge is turning that ambition into reality.

The Port of Lobito handled about 1.8 million tonnes of cargo in 2025, of which around 158,000 tonnes was minerals. Its mineral terminal can handle up to 3.6 million tonnes annually.

"The port already has the capacity," says Neto. "What we need now is sufficient railway capacity and rolling stock."

For residents along the route, the benefits have been immediate.

State-owned railway operator Caminho de Ferro de Benguela (CFB), which runs passenger services, carried a record 1.6 million passengers in 2025.

LAR says it directly employs nearly 1,000 people, 98% of them Angolan.

"This job allows me to support my family," says Domingos Canhama, a father of four and a forklift operator at CFB.

"I can put food on the table, maintain a home and help support my father," he says.

Muzengeza argues that the bigger opportunity is developing local businesses in engineering, maintenance, warehousing and agricultural processing.

"Businesses in Angola need access to the railway, not just proximity to it. That means feeder roads, electricity, storage and predictable services," he says.

But can minerals and their movement along the railways line help deliver peace to eastern DR Congo, where the battle for control of mineral resources has fuelled decades of conflict?

Domingos Canhama, a forklift operator at the Lobido railway company says the job has helped him support his family

Most recently, this has seen the M23 rebel group seize control of many mineral-rich areas.

The Congolese government says neighbouring Rwanda is backing the M23 in order to gain access to DR Congo's mineral wealth. Despite overwhelming evidence, Rwanda denies backing the M23.

The Trump-backed agreements between DR Congo and Rwanda seek to promote transparent mineral supply chains, cross-border investment and regional infrastructure, with the aim of ensuring mineral wealth does not finance armed groups.

"The US wants reliable access to minerals essential to defence, advanced technology and energy systems, while reducing dependence on supply chains in which China has substantial influence," says Muzengeza, the political risk analyst

"Lobito is therefore more than a railway but an infrastructure that can reshape trade routes and commercial power."

At a meeting in Paris in November, DR Congo urged the US to turn its strategic partnership into tangible investments, arguing that success should be measured by completed projects rather than signed agreements.

"We want the partnership to support â local mineral processing, electricity access for households and businesses and peace in the east," said DR Congo's Economy Minister Daniel Mukoko Samba.

Even if more minerals move through formal channels, better infrastructure alone would not resolve the political and security tensions driving the conflict.

There is also the question of who captures the wealth. Much of the copper and cobalt transported from DR Congo through Lobito is destined for processing and manufacturing outside Africa.

While Angola can earn transit fees and create logistics jobs, much of the higher-value activity takes place elsewhere.

"When you export raw materials, you are also exporting jobs and knowledge," Angola's Foreign Affairs Minister TÃ©te AntÃ³nio tells the BBC.

"We should not export lithium but export batteries," he says, arguing that the corridor should help develop local processing and manufacturing rather than simply accelerate raw material exports.

Back in Catumbela, such debates can feel distant from everyday life.

For Julia, the railway already means cheaper journeys and easier movement.

But its ultimate success will depend on whether it can deliver more than faster mineral exports.

Whether it can also help secure lasting peace in eastern DR Congo, where the minerals are mined, remains the bigger test.

How Trump wants the US to cash in on mineral-rich DR Congo's peace deal

#157. ‘I saw a picture of a two-year-old girl buried in mud. I knew we had to do something’ – This is climate breakdown

When the floods hit Pakistan he went out to help. Then the water reached his own family. This is Imran Lodhi’s story

Please enable Javascript to fully experience this interactive article.

Disaster: Pakistan intense monsoon rainfall

Imran Lodhi is a university lecturer with a doctorate in climate change and sustainable development. The 41-year-old was living in Dera Ghazi Khan, a city in south-western Punjab, when floods devastated provinces across Pakistan. Climate change made the monsoon rainfall that caused the floods more intense.

In 2022, I was living in an area that had less risk of flooding. I had a small child and my PhD thesis was just a few weeks away. I tried to avoid flood news on social media until I saw a picture of a two-year-old girl who was buried in mud. It was probably her last breath. She died after that. That was the turning point.

I immediately called my friends, six to seven people, in a mosque. I talked to them: “We don’t know what to do, but at least we can start from somewhere.” Initially, it was food. We hired some commercial cooks.

We planned to work only in villages close to our city. A lot of people were taking refuge in nearby high places, high enough that the water did not reach them. On the first day we went with some food to the nearest village.

When the rain is over, the sun comes out. Usually, it’s a good sign. But, in those areas, it didn’t feel good. It felt like it opened a scar. The pain that was hiding somehow had become open. It was like being naked. I could see trolleys, tractors, vehicles and homes all drowned in the flood. There was nothing from the government, nothing from any development sector. There was no camp. People were just living under open skies.

Men felt that their women being in an open place was a kind of emotional damage for them. It was not normal for most of the people here. Many women never came out. They came out for their work, the agriculture work, but not sitting that openly among the public. Their hair was full of dust and dry mud. Their clothes had a heavy layer of dry mud; no shoes on their feet. Mothers were not breastfeeding. Babies crying for milk, and the milk was not there. Mothers trying to soothe them with fake sounds.

The cows did not have feed and were not giving milk. Many of the animals were lost, their bodies wide open, decomposed. Then the water, which stayed there for a week, started to smell very bad. There was no good smell.

We prepared food in the morning. Then we prepared food at lunchtime. We generally finished distributing food around 8pm. One night it was 11pm and we thought some people might not have food. So we just cooked some food late at night and went to every camp.

One old woman started to laugh. I was surprised. I asked her: “It’s OK, we have food, but why are you laughing?” And she said: “I was just complaining, ‘Lord, that you left me with an empty stomach today.’ And suddenly you appeared.”

It was a very strange experience for her. For us also. People, when they are in tough conditions, are attached to their beliefs. And, when things like that come up, they establish a spiritual connection with the people who are helping out.

We posted on social media. We tried to mobilise. A lot of people contacted us who were from other parts of Pakistan. Money poured in and we kept expanding the team. From two or three villages, we started to cover the entire district, and then almost the entire region.

The government protocols were to not go into certain areas that looked dangerous or were not safe for a boat. Families were trapped, many, many kilometres into the water. There was no land in one village. Small wooden cupboards were hung on trees so stuff could be saved from the water. The immediate shelter was a big tree near them. The whole family was in the tree. The elders were lying on the branches. They somehow made a swing for the kids. They spent days in those trees.

A few villages were completely destroyed. Not a single home was standing. Everything was in shock, even the trees. One old woman who was living with her married kids was not ready to leave that rubble. She was so attached to that home that she felt like there was no life after it. She had a small cupboard in which she kept some small things to sell, for her independence. She was so concerned about that small cupboard. When I reached there, that cupboard was all broken.

One day I had a fever and my wife stopped me: “Don’t go out – you are not well enough to help other people.” But I still went out to a village to help people. When we reached there, I got news that the city we were living in – which I thought was safe – suddenly was a source of threat. There was water coming toward the city. Within hours, the water would be in the city. My family was there. And I was almost 100km away.

So I rushed back. But the road toward the city was cut off by the flood. There was no way I could go back to my family. I called my wife and I asked them to stay vigilant and look out for water in the city. I gave them some suggestions: how they could save themselves and evacuate. That was a very, very tough call.

Eventually, the city was saved. Next day, we took a boat to get past the road that was cut off. Sitting in that boat, I was thinking about my family. Once I reached the other side, I called some people to take me home.

When I reached home, I had a very strong fever and I spent that day at home. I tried to behave normally, but everybody knew I was not feeling well. I took one day off from community work.

The next day I wanted to stop. But the problem was, I was in the middle of that network. If I stop, this whole process will stop. This whole network of relief work will stop.

The next day, even with a high fever, I left for the field because I didn’t want that to stop. When I grew up, the community fabric was very strong. It was common practice that whenever people got sick, got in an accident, hospitalised, people would visit the person and share some money.

This was the kind of culture I observed happening around me.

I went back to those areas where the flood came. There was still some water standing so there were remains of that devastation. But there’s a very common sweet in that area we call jalebi. I can smell that sweet in those villages. I see shops open. I see farmers, labourers. I felt good watching people working again. It looked like life was coming back.

There was an annual poetry event happening three months after the flood. Saraiki culture is very rich, one of the oldest. I was consulted by one of my friends who is the organiser of that event. Should we suspend this year’s events or not?

I said you should never suspend the event. Let people believe the normalisation, let people believe in the spirit of survival. They organised the poetry event.

This is climate breakdown was put together in collaboration with the Climate Disaster Project at the University of Victoria, Canada. Read more.

#155. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#154. Steve Rosenberg: Historic talks with Xi and Trump are a coup for Putin

Nearly five years after the full-scale invasion of Ukraine turned him into a pariah, Putin is back on the global stage

But Thursday's mini summit of Vladimir Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping already looks like a win for the Kremlin.

Think back to 2022. After Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine President Putin was an international pariah. Western leaders were determined to isolate him and his country over the war in Ukraine.

Fast forward to 2026: Vladimir Putin has secured an historic meeting in Shenzhen. For the first time in history, the leaders of China, Russia and the United States will sit down â just the three of them â for talks.

That puts Putin exactly where he wants to be: at the top table of global politics, rubbing shoulders with the leaders of the two great powers.

It allows him to boast that Western pressure on Russia has failed.

"We see such examples in the failed attempts to isolate our country, Russia, which has faced unprecedented sanctions pressure," Putin told a business forum of the Asia-Pacific Economic Co-operation organisation.

That was followed by a meeting with Xi Jinping.

But the Kremlin says the "showstopper" event in Shenzhen will be Putin's trilateral with Xi and Trump.

It allows him to play global statesman, even as the Russian military continues to pound Ukrainian towns and cities, and the Kremlin continues to reject the idea of an unconditional, comprehensive ceasefire.

One short meeting is unlikely to reshape the world. But it's the optics that are key. The picture. The photo-op. Three leaders, three big powers, discussing big issues.

In a way, Vladimir Putin will use this meeting to send a holiday postcard to the rest of the world. Instead of "Wish you were here", he'll sign it: "Look: I'm here."

But what of those who are not at the table?

Among European leaders, and especially in Ukraine, there is concern that the three-way meeting might produce more than just a pretty picture, though not in a good way for Kyiv.

Donald Trump has already said that in Shenzhen he wants to discuss his "favourite subject" - the war in Ukraine. The Kremlin would likely welcome an opportunity to argue its case to Trump and Xi without Ukrainian officials or European leaders in the room.

Last month Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelensky expressed concern that the Putin-Trump-Xi meeting might resemble the Yalta Conference of 1945. That is when the leaders of the Soviet Union, Britain and the United States â Joseph Stalin, Winston Churchill and Franklin D Roosevelt - met to re-organise post-World War Two Europe.

"We really don't want this to turn into 'Yalta 2, 3, 4' with a redistribution of influence," said Zelensky.

It is unclear whether China would want the Ukraine war on the agenda in Shenzhen. In its official statements on the upcoming trilateral meeting, Beijing has talked more generally about the need to discuss "safeguarding world peace and promoting strategic stability".

In Shenzhen, Russia will make the most of the optics. But a diplomatic victory is not the same as a military one.

Nearly five years after President Putin launched his so-called "special military operation", Russia is still struggling to win its war on Ukraine.

Over this time, it has suffered huge losses on the battlefield.

And the Ukrainian strike on the road bridge linking Russia to the Crimean Peninsula â carried out on the eve of the summit - was a blow to Moscow's prestige. The attack severely damaged part of the multi-billion-dollar bridge built by Russia after it annexed Crimea from Ukraine.

The damage is not only a logistical headache for the Russian authorities.

It is a reminder that the Kremlin's "special operation" has not gone at all to plan.

The Shenzhen summit may give Vladimir Putin a big stage, and a bright spotlight.

It does not necessarily bring him closer to winning the war.

#153. A tropical Chinese city is half-empty and ready for the summit.

#152. India has been named a ‘destination of the year’ for 2027 – here are insiders’ tips on travelling beyond the tropes

As tourism booms, three experienced female travellers give their take on how to make the most of travelling here

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

The 13th-century Persian scholar Zakariyya al-Qazwini never travelled to India. But he catalogued it in his famous work The Wonders of Creation and Rarities of Existence, which drew on accounts of a distant land filled with “rarities par excellence”. For al-Qazwini, as Helen Pfeifer writes in the London Review of Books, wonder was not an emotion but an intellectual method of making sense of the world and acquiring knowledge.

India has long occupied such a space in the global imagination, as a land of extraordinary riches; spirituality, customs, chaos and contradictions. This has shaped how the country is perceived from afar, but it can often flatten its complexities.

So when, last week, Travel + Leisure named India its destination of the year for 2027, I found myself considering what it means that the country holds such multitudes. The magazine’s recognition will undoubtedly add to its growing appeal as a travel destination. But expectations built around India can differ from the experience of being there, particularly for women.

My own experiences of travelling in India largely surround my work as a journalist, often visiting places that would never make it into a travel guide. I remember the towering chinar tree outside the home of a woman in Kashmir whose son had been killed in an encounter with the security forces; the rolling hills of Odisha, where we travelled to meet a guerrilla fighter; the tiny hut in Chhattisgarh where, after a 13-hour journey, our team found a meal of rice and lentils. These scenes have stayed in my mind, for their landscapes as much as for the stories and circumstances.

Ultimately, such experiences were far more illuminating than those from a conventional travel itinerary, and taught me to navigate uncertainty and embrace discomfort. For this week’s newsletter, I spoke to three women about the journeys in India that stayed with them and what travelling here has meant to them.

One of the most memorable journeys for travel writer Shivya Nath was a trek through the Tirthan Valley in Himachal Pradesh, with a crew of local women.

Trekking has always been a male-dominated industry and Nath, author of the book Rootless and Restless, was struck by the personal journeys of the women leading the expedition. (She travelled with an organisation which trains women to become guides, overcoming social and family restrictions.) “For some of the women, spending even two nights away from home required negotiating with their families,” she told me.

The route wound through dense forests in the Great Himalayan national park, across ancient bridges, over gushing, undammed rivers, accompanied by the soundtrack of birdsong. “The only sounds you hear are of nature,” she recalled. Along the way, they stopped to forage lingri, a seasonal wild fern used in regional cooking, which they later prepared at a riverside camp they set up for the night.

The following morning, the group hiked to a waterfall, standing together on a large rock in the middle of the cascading water. Being with “just a group of women who love being in the outdoors, was a really powerful feeling for me”, she said.

For Afsha Sethi, a corporate leadership professional from Bengaluru who has travelled extensively in India, one of her most cherished travel experiences is an unlikely one. As part of an extracurricular project when she was in high school, students were paired with a local community through an NGO. She spent a week shadowing a farming family growing rice and areca nut in Karkolli, in northern Karnataka.

Walks through lush forests with waterfalls were a daily routine. “We would come back with leeches on our legs, and then put salt to get rid of them,” she recalled.

The trip also brought her into contact with something she had not encountered before. When her roommate told the host family about getting her period, their sleeping arrangements were shifted to the courtyard. “It turned out to be a great experience, because the weather was very nice and [we were] looking at the stars and just chatting late into the night,” she said. It reminded her that travelling through India can mean encountering different social conventions.

‘The moment somebody puts up something on Instagram, everyone throngs to it’

The travel boom in India is led by domestic travellers. The country recorded almost 2.95bn domestic tourist visits in 2024, a jump of 17.5% from the previous year, according to recent government data. Overall, India ranks 31st in the World Economic Forum’s Travel and Tourism Development Index 2026, up eight spots since 2024.

Women accounted for 43% of foreign arrivals, an interesting counterpoint to India’s reputation as a challenging destination for female travellers.

But the boom raises questions about what happens to the places being discovered. “Social media has definitely accelerated the over-tourism problem,” says Nath. “I think one of the most basic things travellers can do is to avoid geotagging locations.” She suggests choosing community-owned tourism initiatives that allow local people to benefit directly from visitors.

Nayanika Gaffino, a mother and recreational athlete who has also clocked up the miles around the country, has seen this happen first-hand in Goa, where she lives. “The moment somebody puts up something on Instagram, everyone throngs to it … even though it doesn’t have the capacity to handle so many people,” she says, adding there are beaches her family now avoids because of this.

Travelling with girls, Gaffino also has to consider practicalities such as hygiene and access to clean public toilets and safety. As such, she gravitates towards boutique properties that are accustomed to hosting families with children.

Nath acknowledges that solo travel can require extra vigilance. On solo trips, she prefers family-run homestays or guesthouses where someone is aware that she is on her own and can watch out for her. At the same time, she adds, India can be an intensely hospitable place where people generously invite you for tea or a meal.

Discovering a place, she says, should also include finding ways to preserve the very qualities that led you to it in the first place. The journeys the women described involved seeing a side of India that differed from their own expectations. This is what makes al-Qazwini’s understanding of wonder relevant for me. To approach something with wonder is not to be impressed by just beauty or uniqueness but to have a curiosity about what it might show us – its possibilities and its limitations.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Politics | More than one-third of the international delegates – about 115 people – expected to attend the Congress party’s Bharat summit over the weekend were denied entry to India or had their visas cancelled, according to the Wire. Congress described this as an attempt to stifle voices of criticism, while the foreign ministry said that visa-related decisions are “sovereign functions” but did not share further details.

Lawsuit | The US state department has served summons in a civil lawsuit brought by Khalistani separatist leader Gurpatwant Singh Pannu against national security adviser Ajit Doval and former research and analysis wing, Samant Goel, according to the Hindu. The foreign ministry described the lawsuit as “frivolous”.

Culture | A 50-year-old Navaratri festival at Wesleyan University, in Connecticut, has made it an unlikely home for Carnatic music and dance traditions. Although such festivals take place at cities around the world, few institutions have become as significant a platform for Indian music and dance.

A recommendation from someone with great taste

Shaunak Sen, director of the Academy Award-nominated All That Breathes, recommends: “If you find yourself in the oft-trodden window of wanting to watch something for an hour after dinner, but feel drowned in the mediocre flotsam of the big streamers, I have a suggestion watch the strangely inventive short films of the artist-bureaucrat SNS Sastry from the 1960s and 70s [on YouTube]. Unlike the regular pedantic state films of the time, Sastry somehow created avant garde, surreal, even funny films, funded by the Films Division of India. I Am 20, which interviewed young adults born in the year of India’s independence, is an inquiry into a certain national zeitgeist. Between the candour of the participants and the sharpness of their complaints, one can scarcely believe this was made under the aegis of the government.”

Firecrackers were responsible for most of Delhi’s fine particulate matter of PM 2.5 – about 71% – in the hours after Diwali celebrations, according to a first-of-its-kind assessment by the Indian Institute of Tropical Meteorology. The national capital reported its most-polluted Diwali since 2021, data from the Central Pollution Control Board showed.

AI anxiety is everywhere. Pranav Bhasin’s comedy We Were Here has brought that to screens in a short film that is making an impression internationally. Bhasin’s mockumentary is about three retired men in a small Indian town who, persuaded that artificial intelligence is taking over human jobs, fight back by impersonating household appliances. One becomes a dish antenna, another a vehicle’s reverse camera and a third a microwave. The situation becomes more complicated when one of their children takes a job at an AI company. “Do you want artificial intelligence?” “No, I have real one,” goes one caustic exchange.

The New Yorker released the film on YouTube and it’s eligible for Oscars consideration after winning at the Palm Springs International ShortFest.

#151. US Pentagon chief Hegseth signs military deal in Venezuela

On a visit to Caracas, Pete Hegseth signed a deal that signifies the "reestablishment of our defense partnership." US-Venezuela ties were strained for years until US troops ousted ex-President Nicolas Maduro in January.

US Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth visited Venezuela on Wednesday, met with the country's interim authorities and also signed a military cooperation deal with the Latin American country.

The Pentagon did not release any information on Hegseth's trip or confirm the visit, although it was broadcast on Venezuelan state media.

The visit comes after US forces in January ousted former Venezuelan President Nicolas Maduro and took him along with his wife to New York to face drug trafficking charges. Since then, US President Donald Trump has backed acting President Delcy Rodriguez to lead the country.

Both Rodriguez and Hegseth touted the new military agreement between the two countries at the presidential palace in Caracas.

Rodriguez said the deal would strengthen training for the Venezuelan military while facilitating access to new technologies. She said this would allow the two nations to "face common threats."

Hegseth said the US sees the deal "as a reestablishment of our defense partnership and the opportunity to work together between our two great militaries."

The agreement includes intelligence sharing between the two nations in addition to joint training.

The Pentagon chief said the US seeks "to ensure the Venezuelan people are not threatened by foreign actors or narco-terrorists or criminals and that they are safe and secure inside sovereign borders."

Hegseth said Wednesday that since Maduro's ouster, Venezuela "has gone from our most aggressive regional threat to a compliant security partner."

US-Venezuela relations were strained under Maduro, who had taken office in 2013. In 2019, Maduro cut off Venezuela's formal diplomatic relations with the US after Trump's first administration recognized Venezuelan opposition leader Juan Guaido as the country's interim president.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

After the ouster of Maduro, the Trump administration urged Venezuela to comply with demands such as US access to the Venezuelan oil market. Trump had threatened Rodriguez, who was Venezuela's vice president, with a "fate worse than Maduro" if she didn't cooperate with Washington.

Trump has later praised Rodriguez as a "terrific person" who is doing a "great job."

Under Rodriguez, Venezuela has released political prisoners and facilitated the shipments of oil to the US, with the US then controlling the proceeds and returning the money under an energy deal. US Secretary of State Marco Rubio has been overseeing Venezuela's transition and is in close contact with Rodriguez.

Elections after Maduro's ouster are expected at some point in Venezuela, but with Rodriguez's government acting favorably towards the US, it's unclear whether they would happen anytime soon. Nobel Prize winner and opposition leader Maria Corina Machado recently urged elections after arriving in Venezuela.

#150. Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

It was during November 2020 that somewhere in San Francisco, a woman called Daniela Amodei phoned a friend, asking for advice.

Daniela oversaw safety and policy at OpenAI, one of the most talked-about AI companies in the world.

But Daniela and her brother Dario - who worked there too - had misgivings over safety levels at OpenAI and thought it could be time to walk away.

So she did what many do when we can't make a decision about something, she phoned a friend. The friend's reply was direct and to the point: "You don't need to be on the phone with me. You already know the answer."

The Amodei siblings and a handful of other colleagues quit. Together, they set up a rival â Anthropic.

Six years on and Anthropic, the maker of leading AI model Claude, has predicted revenues of $52bn in 2026 and $135bn in 2027, according to analyst estimates.

It is about to sell shares to the public for the first time in what's known as an Initial Public Offering or IPO, aiming for a valuation of an estimated $1.4tn (Â£1tn), which would propel it straight into the top 10 of the most valuable US companies.

The sale comes at a crucial time when many are wondering whether, amid increasing concern about the potential of AI, the industry's bubble is showing signs of bursting.

Where once investors were "all in" on a technological revolution to rival the railroads, the car, electricity and the internet, some are now hedging their bets, amid arguments that AI prices will be driven down as the technology becomes more common and concerns that a hefty wedge of the AI investment is tech giants investing in each other.

Shares in companies that supply the raw computing power behind AI, like Coreweave and Nebius, have seen their value cut in half while even the mighty Nvidia has lost its crown as the world's most valuable company with its shares down 30% - a staggering $2tn fall in value.

So could the Anthropic IPO reinflate the bubble or will it be remembered as the last gasp of a boom that is heading for a bust?

At an event last week, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei told JPMorgan Chase's CEO Jamie Dimon that his guiding principle was to "never let your company be worth a single dollar more than it's worth".

The company is selling roughly $65bn worth of shares to the general public and a further $26bn on to other big tech companies. It is comfortably the single biggest money raising event in US corporate history.

It's an old adage that during a gold rush you should buy companies that sell the picks and shovels as opposed to those staking everything on striking gold. In AI's case that's the chip makers, the data centre builders, the energy providers. But Anthropic is arguably the gold itself and this IPO will see what value people put on it, according to Fiona Cincotta.

"I think the reason that this IPO is so important is basically it's the first ability for the public investors to access a pure play AI trade," she says, adding that previous offerings have been about "other parts of the ecosystem, which are, the semiconductors, the chips, the, data centres. But this is the first time that we were actually able to access the AI provider."

Daniela Amodei helped set up a rival company to OpenAI

What a company is worth depends almost entirely on predicted future earnings.

It's that calculation that many think presents the sharpest needle to the AI bubble.

Will current and future users of AI be prepared to pay enough to justify the trillions being spent on the chips, data centres, energy and employees, some of whom can command annual salaries of over $100m?

"AI is certainly in a financial bubble. While LLMs [large language models] offer genuine utility and are undoubtedly here to stay, the economics don't add up," said Gary Marcus, Professor Emeritus at New York University and author of Taming Silicon Valley, speaking earlier this year.

"[The] more companies build AI models, the [more the] technology becomes commoditised, eliminating meaningful competitive advantages and driving down prices."

So far, the growth rates in Anthropic revenue are extraordinary.

But these explosive growth rates hide a potential problem. Around a quarter of that revenue came from just a handful of companies, among them Google and Amazon.

It's also concentrated in the US which accounts for two-thirds of its revenue.

There is also the staggering network of circular financing found in the AI world. For example, Anthropic has agreements to buy or lease hundreds of billions of dollars worth of chips and computing power from a handful of companies including Broadcom, Google, Amazon. Many of those firms are also investors in Anthropic and major customers of its products.

It's not just some Wall Street investors questioning whether the AI boom is all it's cracked up to be. Out on Main Street, ordinary Americans have concerns too.

With the US driving so much of global AI growth, a growing public backlash against a technology that threatens to replace millions of jobs and hoover up vast amounts of energy and water became a factor in the recent midterm elections in the US.

Many areas in the US have passed moratoriums on data centre development and many candidates deemed too close to big tech found that association a political liability rather than an asset. Many Democrat candidates publicly opposed data centre development and won.

Added to that, the Pied Piper of AI, chip maker Nvidia's CEO Jensen Huang, resigned last week after admitting to having been "unfaithful" to his wife after reports he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang.

Dario Amodei is one of Silicon Valley's big beasts

Huang's spectacular fall from grace and Nvidia's 30% fall in value from its peak have left AI investors looking for a new champion for the AI boom that has underpinned the US stock market and arguably much of its entire economy for the last two years.

If Anthropic â and its CEO are to be AI's new Pied Piper, he will be playing a different tune to the relentlessly upbeat, leather jacket-clad talisman Huang.

While Huang wanted AI development to continue full throttle, Anthropic was built on misgivings over the levels of safety and responsibility the OpenAI leavers felt were being exercised over the development of society-changing technology.

Amodei has warned that AI capabilities could enable biological weapon development, mass surveillance, authoritarianism and the replacement of millions of white-collar, tax-paying workers.

He has called for a slowdown in its development as its powers are growing at such an exponential rate that it could very soon elude any human oversight.

That said, his paternalistic overtures have been sceptically received by those who point out that the pace of Anthropic's development of new more powerful models has shown no sign of the slowdown he's advocating for the rest of the industry.

But for all the concerns, just as with Elon Musk's SpaceX before it â the hype and the FOMO (fear of missing out) on an undoubtedly profound moment in technological and perhaps human development â means that buying interest is high.

It's reported that orders to buy shares exceed the number on offer by over six times.

With demand outstripping supply by such a margin, a first-day jump is likely given the oversubscription, but it doesn't tell you much about long-run return.

Using SpaceX as an example again, shares initially rose nearly 70% from its IPO sale price of $135 to $225. Shares in SpaceX are currently back to $145.

The Anthropic IPO could be wildly successful and blow a bit of fresh new air into what some maintain still looks a lot like a bubble.

Eileen Burbidge, founder of tech investors Passion Capital, is one of those who reject the notion that AI is a bubble about to burst.

"Maybe the growth of the bubble has slowed down a little bit, but actually I wouldn't be willing to say that it's about to burst yet," says Burbidge. "I think this IPO could actually mean everyone could be off to the races again."

Fiona Cincotta believes Anthropic's IPO is about far more than just Anthropic. "I think what's going to be really interesting here is I think it can actually be used as a valuation benchmark for the entire AI ecosystem," she says.

"That's why I don't think this is just going to affect the share price of Anthropic. I think this is going to have much wider implications across the whole AI trade."

Two things can be true at the same time. The Anthropic IPO could be wildly successful - FOMO is a powerful emotion in financial markets. But its probable success won't soothe the unease in some quarters that not every company, every investor and every citizen will think of AI as the answer to humanity's problems.
