#149. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#146. Palestinian woman shot dead by Israeli settlers near Jericho, witnesses say

Bullet hit 72-year-old Fadha Wadha in head outside her home in al-Duyuk al-Tahta, in the West Bank, say residents

A 72-year-old Palestinian woman has been shot dead in an overnight attack by Israeli settlers in a village near Jericho where settlers have recently moved in after taking over several Palestinian homes.

Fadha Wadha was standing outside her home in al-Duyuk al-Tahta in the occupied West Bank when she was struck in the head by a bullet as Israeli settlers fired towards Palestinian houses, according to testimonies from residents. Several other Palestinians, including her son, were wounded by gunfire.

Abu Younis, a cousin of Wadha’s husband, told the Guardian that the violence had begun when a group of settlers attacked a young boy they accused of entering a courtyard seized from its Palestinian residents.

Dozens of Israeli settlers, some of them armed, moved into eight buildings in al-Duyuk al-Tahta two weeks ago with the backing of the settler organisation Nachala and a regional council representing illegal settlements in the central West Bank.

“A vehicle carrying settlers chased him,” Younis said. “There were about 15 settlers in several vehicles. They began firing indiscriminately, killing the woman.”

Witnesses said Palestinian residents began to throw stones to defend themselves, as more settlers arrived at the scene, shooting at civilians and injuring six men.

Wadha’s son, Arafat Fidhat, was wounded after he rushed to the scene after learning that his mother had been shot. His wife, Naama Fidhat, told Reuters he was in shock when he accidentally collided with a parked car belonging to one of the settlers.

Witnesses said the vehicle was empty at the time, but that settlers responded by opening fire, wounding Arafat in the torso.

Naama Fidhat said her husband was being taken to a hospital in Ramallah when Israeli soldiers stopped the ambulance and arrested him. She said Israeli soldiers also arrested their son, Wadha’s grandson, early the following morning.

“What did we do? We didn’t do anything,” she said. “This is our house, they were the ones who came with weapons … we were unarmed, we were sleeping.”

The Israeli military said a group of Palestinians in ​a car “accelerated their vehicle toward an Israeli vehicle and rammed it”. It said the Palestinians exited the vehicle and attacked the Israelis, who opened fire at the Palestinians. “There are Israeli and Palestinian ​casualties in the incident,” the military said.

The arrival of the Israeli settlers in al-Duyuk al-Tahta had raised fears among Palestinian residents who have faced repeated attacks in the area.

The Binyamin regional council, which covers 47 illegal settlements, claimed the properties in the village had been bought from Palestinians, but Jericho’s governor, Hussein Hamayel, disputed the claim, saying the land belonged to the state and could not legally be sold.

Human rights activists said settlers had assaulted and robbed two Israeli activists and shot a Palestinian man in the leg during the takeover earlier this month.

“Since the settlers moved into the area on 4 November our lives have been turned upside down,” Younis told the Guardian. “We used to live peacefully and safely on our farms, but since the settlers arrived, our lives have become a living hell. I own 53 dunams [5.3 hectares] of farmland with greenhouses, but we are no longer allowed to access them. We have suffered devastating financial losses, and now people are being killed as well.”

There has been a sharp ​rise in attacks by Israeli settlers on Palestinians in the West Bank, territory Israel captured in the 1967 six-day war. UN bodies and most ​countries deem the settlements illegal, which Israel disputes.

On Tuesday, Suleiman Abu Srour, a photographer for the Palestinian news agency Wafa, was beaten by Israeli soldiers while covering events in al-Duyuk al-Tahta. Wafa said he suffered fractures and bruises, was detained for six hours and had his phone confiscated before being released.

Since the 7 October 2023 Hamas-led attacks on Israel, there has been a surge of Israeli settlement expansion, ethnic cleansing and violence against Palestinians in the occupied West Bank. More than 1,100 Palestinians have been killed there by Israeli forces or settlers.

#145. India's religious make-up - what a new Pew study says about Hindus and Muslims

An Indian woman lights lamps on the occasion of Diwali, the Hindu festival of lights

India will remain overwhelmingly Hindu for decades to come, but its religious composition is projected to shift gradually.

A new study by the Pew Research Center, projecting the world's religious populations to 2070, estimates that Hindus will make up 76% of India's population by then, down from 79% in 2020, while the Muslim share will rise from 15% to 20%.

The shift is rooted largely in differences in age and fertility: Indian Muslims are younger than Hindus and, on average, have more children.

The Pew projections place India at the centre of a wider demographic shift across South Asia.

Pakistan is expected to overtake Indonesia as the country with the world's largest Muslim-majority population, with India moving into second place. Bangladesh, too, is projected to see substantial growth in its Muslim population.

The study projects the religious composition of the world over the next 50-year period, drawing on data from 201 countries and territories, covering virtually the entire global population.

Muslims offer prayer at the historic Jama Masjid in Delhi

It tracks seven religious groups between 2020 and 2070, assuming recent demographic trends continue. Using census and survey data, it establishes each group's size and age profile in 2020, and factors in differences in fertility and religious switching.

The findings place India at the heart of a changing global religious landscape. The country is expected to have the world's second-largest Muslim population by 2070, after Pakistan, with about 334 million Muslims. Pakistan itself will overtake Indonesia as the country with the largest Muslim population, with an estimated 461 million.

Yet the projections offer little support for the more dramatic narratives sometimes attached to India's changing religious composition.

If current demographic trends continue, the Hindu population in the country is expected to grow by 176 million between 2020 and 2070, the report says. It will continue to account for more than three-quarters of the country's population.

Despite a sharp fall in India's overall fertility, Muslim women still have more children, on average, than Hindu women. Pew attributes the shift primarily to demographic momentum.

Indian Muslims had a median age of 24 in 2020, compared with 29 for Hindus.

Between 2025 and 2030, Pew estimates Muslim women in India will have an average of 2.3 children, against 1.8 among Hindu women.

"We expect religious change in India to be driven almost entirely by long-standing demographic differences between Hindus and Muslims: Muslims are younger and have higher fertility rates," Stephanie Kramer of the Pew Research Center told the BBC.

Hindu devotees offer prayers on the banks of the Sabarmati river in India's Gujarat

"The fertility gap has narrowed considerably â from more than one child per woman in 1992 to about half a child in recent years and is likely to keep shrinking," she said.

"But Hindu fertility is already below the replacement level, while Muslim fertility is projected to remain above it for another two decades." (A replacement level is one at which new births are sufficient to maintain a stable population.)

The projections are hardly surprising, says KS James, an Indian demographer at Princeton University.

"The fertility gap between Muslims and Hindus is well established and it means Muslim population growth is likely to remain faster for the next few decades. But fertility is declining across all religious communities and the gap is expected to narrow further," he told the BBC.

"Ultimately, they will converge," he says, adding that the projections do not point to anything dramatically different in India's demographic trajectory.

Demographers push back against claims, often amplified by Hindu right-wing groups, that Muslims will eventually outnumber Hindus. There is no evidence of any significant religious conversion driving population change in India, they say, and the projections do not support such "alarmist scenarios".

Muslim children dressed in festive attire in India

The wider South Asian picture is equally revealing, according to the report.

Pakistan's Muslim population is projected to more than double to 461 million by 2070, overtaking Indonesia as the world's largest. India will rank second, with 334 million Muslims, while Bangladesh's Muslim population will rise to 218 million. India and Nepal, meanwhile, will remain the world's only Hindu-majority countries.

There appears to be a global story behind these regional shifts.

By 2035, Muslims are projected to overtake Christians as the world's largest religious group, according to the report. By 2070, their numbers are expected to reach 3.64 billion, up from 2.02 billion in 2020.

Christianity, meanwhile, will have just under three billion followers. Muslims will account for 36% of humanity, compared with 26% today.

But the most dramatic transformation may be taking place thousands of miles west of South Asia, in Africa.

Christianity's centre of gravity is moving steadily away from Europe and the Americas towards sub-Saharan Africa.

The report says that if recent trends continue, more than half of the world's Christians are expected to live there by 2070, compared with 31% in 2020. Pew forecasts that the Democratic Republic of Congo will overtake the United States as the country with the largest Christian population.

With more than 1.4 billion people, India overtook China in 2023 in terms of population, according to the UN

The steepest decline is expected to occur in Europe. If current trends continue, by 2070 Europe will be home to 11% of the world's Christian population, down from 22% in 2020, the report says.

The US is projected to lose its Christian majority by 2065, although Christians will remain the country's largest religious group in 2070.

One reason for this changing religious landscape is the growing number of people identifying with no religion.

In UK and France, the "religiously unaffiliated" are projected to become the majority by 2070. South Korea is expected to follow suit.

Yet their share of the world's population is forecast to fall from 24% to 17%, largely because of a decline in China's population.

China accounts for about two-thirds of the world's religiously unaffiliated population. Its expected population decline will more than offset growth in secular populations elsewhere, the report says.

By 2070, there are other unexpected shifts.

The US is projected to become the world's fifth-largest Hindu population, with immigration driving much of its growth.

And Australia's unaffiliated population will overtake Christians as the country's largest group.

Buddhism, meanwhile, faces a demographic squeeze. Its population is expected to fall by more than half in China, Japan and South Korea, reflecting the ageing and shrinking populations of East Asia. Laos and Mongolia are projected to lose their Buddhist majorities.

There are, of course, caveats. These are demographic projections, not predictions of everything that might happen. Pew assumes that recently observed patterns of fertility, migration and religious switching - or conversions - will continue.

"There are many events that could affect religious group populations (including wars, natural disasters, epidemics and changes in economic or social circumstances) that are not analysed here," the report admits.

India's baseline also required projecting the 2020 religious composition from the 2011 census, because the subsequent census had been delayed

Still, the broad direction is unmistakable.

South Asia will remain indispensable to the world's religious story, not just because of India's enormous Hindu population but because of the growing weight of its Muslim populations. Africa will increasingly shape Christianity's future, while Europe and parts of East Asia become more secular.

Clearly, by 2070, the world's religious landscape may look markedly different, shaped less by conversion than by the uneven arithmetic of births, deaths and migration.

#144. What the Crimean bridge attack means for Russia

Ukraine's latest strike on the Kerch Bridge has disrupted a key transport link between Russia and Crimea. Beyond the damage, the attack could have serious consequences for Russian military logistics and Putin's prestige.

It had been a year and a half since Ukraine last attacked the 19-kilometer-long Kerch Bridge connecting Russian-annexed Crimea with Russia's Krasnodar region.

Now, Ukraine has struck again. The attack during the night of November 17 caused the highway section of the bridge to collapse into the sea. As a result, shipping traffic between the Black Sea and the Sea of Azov has also been disrupted.

Russia's ministry of transport announced a "temporary suspension" of road and rail traffic on the bridge, though later, rail services were resumed. Russian authorities have launched a criminal investigation into what they are calling a "terrorist attack." It was only after this announcement that the Security Service of Ukraine (SBU) confirmed the attack.

The Kerch Strait bridge is considered one of Russian President Vladimir Putin's most prestigious projects. Its construction began after Russia's 2014 annexation of the Ukrainian Crimea peninsula, an act considered illegal under international law. The Kerch Bridge was completed four years later.

It has been targeted by Ukraine on several occasions since Russia's 2022 full-scale invasion. The most well-known strike occurred in October 2022, when an explosives-laden truck detonated on the bridge, causing extensive damage and several months of traffic restrictions. The most recent attack took place in June 2025, when the SBU reported that an underwater support structure was blown up.

The latest Ukrainian operation lasted about a month, and consisted of seven distinct phases. "First, the SBU and armed forces systematically neutralized the enemy's air defense system. After that, the direct attack on the bridge began," says the SBU Telegram channel.

Some Russian military bloggers claim that the attack was carried out with the support of Ukraine's Western allies, or at least using their equipment and weapons.

Ukraine has not confirmed this. It has instead pointed out that various Ukrainian-made weapons were used in the attack, ranging from a variety of drones, to FP-5 Flamingo and R-360 Neptun missiles. British daily The Times has cited Ukrainian military sources, saying that Ukraine attacked the bridge without the support of SpaceX's satellite network system Starlink. There has been no confirmation of this so far.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, meanwhile, described the strike as a response to Russian attacks: "Russia is attacking our infrastructure every day, particularly bridges in Zaporizhzhia, Dnipro, Kyiv, and the Odesa region." He said "we are rebuilding our infrastructure, but we also remind Russia that the war always returns to where it began."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

After the attack, pictures and videos of the bridge were shared on social media. The Krimskij Veter Telegram channel has highlighted damage to the railroad link and questioned why Russian authorities have opted to resume train services.

The Kerch Strait shipping channel is also completely blocked by debris. "This brings road traffic to a standstill, but also shipping between the Black Sea and the Sea of Azov," the channel writes.

Indeed, the Kerch Strait navigational channel is merely 120 meters wide, writes Mychajlo Hontschar of the Ukrainian Center for Global Studies "Strategy XXI" on Facebook. "The strait is shallow, only three to five meters deep."

As early as the 19th century, the seabed was dredged to allow heavier ships to pass from the Black Sea to the Sea of Azov and vice versa. "Now, Vladimir Putin's bridge-building efforts have brought shipping to an indefinite standstill," Hontschar said.

Not only will river-sea vessels no longer be able to leave the waters of the Sea of Azov, the Don, and the Volga, but boats and small ships from the Black Sea fleet and the Caspian flotilla based in Novorossiysk will also be unable to do so.

Many pro-Kremlin Telegram channels have been quick to claim that the bridge does not play an important role in the Russian army's logistics or in supplying Crimea.

The Dva Mayora (Two Majors) channel quotes the "Deputy President of the Republic of Crimea," Olga Kurlayeva, who described the bridge as a "purely civilian structure."

Ukrainian military experts interviewed by DW, on the other hand, point to the systematic disruption of Russian logistics. These efforts also included regular attacks on the R-80 highway leading through Russian-occupied territories of the Donetsk, Zaporizhzhia, and Kherson regions.

Russia cannot replace the Kerch Bridge with any other land connection, says Oleksandr Kovalenko, a Ukrainian military expert with the Information Resistance Group that seeks to counter disinformation. He expects the damaged bridge will cause supply problems for the Russian armed forces: "If there is a resource shortage during attacks or in defense, then either the attacks or the defense will fail."

That is why he does not rule out a decline in attacks from occupied Crimea on the rest of Ukraine. "There are many launch pads for kamikaze drones in Crimea — in the Hvardijsk area, at Cape Chauda and in Balaklava," Kovalenko told DW. "From there, many drones are launched toward Odesa, Mykolaiv, and the right bank of the Dnipro River in the Kherson region. They also fly over Crimea en route toward Ukraine. Then there are attacks using Iskander-M ballistic missiles, as well as Zircon and Oniks missiles, guided missiles, and glide bombs. And while they don't originate in Crimea, they are brought over via the Kerch Bridge."

Kovalenko says the bridge strike has dealt a serious blow to Vladimir Putin's standing. It comes at a sensitive time as Putin is about to meet Chinese President Xi Jinping and US President Donald Trump in Shenzhen, China.

Memes and witty comments about the bridge abound on X. Meanwhile, the Russian president's spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has promised to rebuild the bridge and assured that it will be "even better than before."

Andrii Korezkyj of the Kyiv University of Transportation, however, says this is not likely to happen anytime soon. "All in all, I would estimate that it would take between one and a half and three years [to rebuild]," Korezkyj told DW, adding, "I don't think they'll let the Russians build another [bridge] arch like that near the Ukrainian border without interference."

Pro-Russian commentators, known as Z-bloggers, agree with this view. "Unlike in 2022 and 2023, the Ukrainian armed forces are now able to launch strikes against floating cranes and repair teams."

"Even installing a pre-assembled arch under these conditions would constitute a challenging task," writes the pro-Kremlin Rybar Telegram channel.

#142. Israeli parliament elects new speaker backed by Netanyahu’s opponents

Knesset’s appointment of Matan Kahana marks step towards reversing policies of outgoing government

The Israeli parliament elected a new speaker on Tuesday, a key step toward rolling back policies passed by the outgoing government led by the prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu.

The new speaker is Matan Kahana, an ally of Gadi Eisenkot, the party leader currently working to form the next government and replace Netanyahu.

Eisenkot’s bloc has pledged to undo some of Netanyahu’s most contentious policies following Kahana’s election.

He was approved as speaker with 66 votes in the 120-member chamber, backed by the Jewish-led opposition parties and by the Arab-led parties. The candidate backed by Netanyahu’s bloc, Ofir Katz, received 52 votes following a stormy debate that stretched over seven hours.

“The appointment of Matan Kahana as Knesset speaker is a worthy democratic move that reflects the absolute majority in favour of change,” Eisenkot said ahead of the vote.

The vote took place weeks after the parliamentary elections on 27 October in which Netanyahu and his allies lost their parliamentary majority. Eisenkot was subsequently tasked by Israel’s president with trying to form a new coalition government.

Eisenkot has up to five more weeks to secure enough alliances to form a coalition.

As negotiations continue on the sidelines, his bloc, along with unaffiliated Arab-led parties, can use their majority in the newly sworn-in Knesset to advance their legislative agenda.

They introduced several bills last week, including to set term limits on the role of prime minister, to form a government-led inquiry into the Hamas-led 7 October 2023 attacks and to reverse laws that limit the powers of the judiciary.

Kahana gives the opposition bloc a major legislative advantage, according to Assaf Shapira of the Israel Democracy Institute, a Jerusalem thinktank.

“It’s important because there is not much time, because the speaker of the Knesset can make the legislative process much, much faster or much slower,” Shapira said.

Kahana replaced Amir Ohana, a Netanyahu ally, as speaker.

Eisenkot and his allies are under pressure to swiftly advance their legislative priorities in the weeks ahead. Roy Folkman, a member of Eisenkot’s Yashar party, heads the temporary committee that decides much of the Knesset’s work until a government is formed.

If Eisenkot fails to form a government, the Israeli president could give Netanyahu an opportunity to do so. That would probably give Netanyahu’s Likud party control of the committee.

Even though Netanyahu’s opponents would still hold a majority, they would “feel that their momentum has gone”, Shapira said, and there would be a “real danger that some of the legislation will be delayed”.

Chamber procedures will pose a further challenge. A bill has to be introduced on the floor, considered in committee and voted on several times on the floor before it passes the Knesset. That winding process could make it hard to move bills across the finish line before time runs out.

Shapira said Eisenkot and his allies are likely to prioritise rolling back controversial laws that gave the government a larger role in appointing judges and weakened the power of Israel’s attorney general, an independent role that is seen by many as central to Israel’s system of government checks and balances.

“It’s very easy to abolish these laws because no new institution has to be created,” he said.

Establishing a body to investigate the 7 October attacks would be easier, Shapira added, and carry greater legitimacy once a government is formed.

Netanyahu’s opponents argue he has blocked an independent probe into any military or intelligence failures around the day, the deadliest in Israel’s history, and evaded accountability.

#141. 'The drones don't fly here' - BBC reports from Ukraine's new front line

Watch: Inside the gas pipeline where Ukraine is fighting an underground war with Russia

There's a new front in the war in Ukraine, and it is underground.

For the past seven months Ukrainian forces have been living and fighting Russian troops in an old Soviet gas pipeline in the north-eastern Kharkiv region.

With a diameter of just over a metre there is enough room only to crawl. But it is relatively safe from the drones that now saturate the skies above the front line â and make it nigh-on impossible for either side to advance.

Going underground is now the "only option", says Zanuda, who has helped plan Ukraine's tunnel operation and calls it a new world of fighting. But, he acknowledges, it is hard.

"On the surface you always have some room to manoeuvre, but here you can only engage the enemy head-on."

The journey to get to this subterranean battleground in the Kharkiv region only reinforced the dangers on the ground: a high-speed move by vehicle across open fields, followed by a long march across muddy ground â accompanied by Ukrainian troops with their weapons loaded, ready to shoot down Russian drones.

Arriving at the tunnel entrance, the first thing to hit you is the lingering smell of gas and fetid air.

Both sides use openings in the pipeline to get to the surface, where most of the fighting takes place. They have made breaches in the ground, and used welding tools to break through the thick steel.

The Russians were the first to exploit the pipe to infiltrate the nearby city of Kupyansk in September 2025. They have used underground pipelines before, during the capture of the city of Avdiivka in 2024, and last year in their bid to recapture areas of Russia's Kursk region.

But Ukrainian forces have slowly turned the tables.

Troops move through the tunnel on special sleds - they call it the Kupyansk metro

A specially designed electric cart transports troops and supplies through the pipeline.

They call it the Kupyansk metro. It occasionally breaks down. We travelled on it for 40 minutes, heads bowed and legs crossed. There is a regular bump as you cross the welded sections of the pipe. It is like an austere fairground ride, without the fun.

Our destination was a break in the pipeline where Ukrainian troops have built dug-outs to store ammunition and equipment, with just enough room to stand. It is where the man they call Musician conducts the movement of troops and supplies. He has been living like a mole, his face engrained with dirt and earth.

Musician tells me he has spent the past three months there, with a few brief visits above ground to clean up or go to the toilet. From time to time we can hear the thuds of artillery rounds above. Musician tells me we are close to the line of contact: "But it's safe underground."

Ukrainian troops like Musician rarely see the sun - but they say they feel safer underground

The cart continues its journey into the bowels of the earth. It returns about half an hour later with more troops covered in dirt. They have been fighting in positions on the surface.

There is a look of relief on their dust-caked faces.

"It's more comfortable in the pipe because drones don't fly here, and neither do artillery shells or glide bombs," says Vovochka, who has been fighting on the ground for the past three weeks.

But there has been fighting in the pipeline, too. Operation Veles, named after a mythical Slavic god of the underworld, began in April. Since then Ukraine has advanced 15km (9.3 miles) along the pipeline.

It has been painstaking, backbreaking work â with the ever-present fear of coming into contact with the enemy.

Back above ground we meet a few of the soldiers, from the 13th Khartiia Brigade of Ukraine's National Guard, who have taken part in the mission. Their first task was to clear the detritus, water and Russian barricades inside the pipeline.

The Ukrainian troops say some of the barriers were boobytrapped or piled with Russian bodies. "When we moved them or climbed over them, we had no idea what was underneath," says Chyzh.

The other danger, he said, was the ricochets from gunfire when they tried to advance. "You never know where a bullet is going to go or how it will bounce."

His comrade Kort says "we were all firing, we were pushing them back". Over the course of the operation Ukraine says it killed more than 100 Russian soldiers and took 30 prisoners. "Yes, there were bodies," says Kort. "I even had to sleep right next to them."

The men live and fight in very tight quarters - but have managed to clear 15km of the pipe

Their commander's former life as a miner helped prepare them for the physical and psychological challenges of operating underground.

From a building on the surface, Biashym keeps in regular contact with his men still in the pipeline. Behind him is a cabinet full of medicine.

Soldiers in the pipeline spend much of their time on their knees and suffer abrasions. Biashym admits some have had panic attacks from living in such a confined space. Others have struggled to breathe.

Hundreds of Colombian volunteers have also been involved in the operation, part of the RUG 23rd Assault Regiment.

Foreign fighters have helped boost Ukraine's depleted ranks. One of the Colombians, whose call sign is Gecko, tells me it took them 30 days to just clear 500m.

"It was difficult, it was hot and I had fear inside the pipe," he said. "But at some point I just thought, OK, just deal with it and let's advance."

Russian forces, like Ukraine's, have built fortified positions inside the pipeline. They still try to infiltrate Ukrainian positions, burrowing their way in and out of the pipe. From a control room above ground we watched Ukrainian drones target another Russian attempt to outflank them.

While some of the fighting taking place underground, there are still battles on the surface and in the air. It has been a combined operation.

But Zanuda, who is chief of the planning section of the 13th Khartiia Brigade, believes it is the best way to fight in the future. "Ground operations are now nearly impossible," he said, adding that "everyone understands, for now, the only way to fight is underground".

There'll be more missions like this, he believes. "Pipelines run everywhere," he said. "You have to remember, back in the Soviet times, oil, gas and ammonia were being transported to Europe, with the entire transit route running through Ukraine."

In reality it has been brutal, gruelling and dangerous work in the harshest of conditions.

It has taken Ukraine seven months to clear 15km of the pipeline.

Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelensky has hailed it as a success.

It has helped retake lost territory and been a boost for morale, but on its own it has not changed the course of the war.

Additional reporting by Moose Campbell, Volodymyr Lozhko and Rebecca Hartmann

#139. Pakistani man sentenced to 27 years in US prison over Trump murder plot

Asif Merchant, allegedly linked to Iran, found by court to have sought retribution over Qassem Suleimani death

A Pakistani man was sentenced to more than 27 years in prison on Tuesday for plotting to assassinate Donald Trump in retaliation for the US killing of Revolutionary Guards commander Qassem Suleimani, the Department of Justice said.

Asif Merchant, who had alleged ties to Iran, sought to hire a hitman to assassinate a politician or a government official in the United States, a Brooklyn court found.

Suleimani, the head of Iran’s foreign military operations, was killed in a US drone strike in Baghdad in January 2020. Iranian officials have repeatedly vowed to avenge his killing.

Merchant had testified that he was forced into the plot to protect his family in the Iranian capital Tehran from the Guards, adding that he thought he would get caught before anyone was killed, multiple media outlets reported.

He said he was never ordered to kill a specific person but noted his Iranian contact had mentioned three people in connection with the plot – Trump, the former US president Joe Biden and former UN ambassador Nikki Haley.

But Todd Blanche, the United States attorney general, insisted on Tuesday that the plot was against Trump.

“An Iranian agent who came to the United States to coordinate an assassination attempt against President Trump has been justly sentenced for his criminal conduct,” Blanche said after Merchant was sentenced to 330 months’ imprisonment.

“The Department of Justice will not tolerate foreign terrorist plots against our leaders and will not stop until those responsible are held accountable.”

US officials previously said Merchant had “close ties to Iran” and described his alleged plot as “straight out of the Iranian regime’s playbook”.

Merchant was arrested on 12 July 2024 as he planned to leave the country.

#138. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#135. Grand Theft Auto VI launches to global fanfare

After 13 years of waiting, fans around the world will get to play the latest installment in the blockbuster video game series. In focus: A tale of crime and violence in a fictionalized version of Miami.

Grand Theft Auto VI officially launched Thursday, ending a 13-year wait for one of the most anticipated games ever.

The game became playable at midnight local time, depending on location, on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X, meaning gamers in Asia will get to play the game ahead of users in the US and Europe.

In New Zealand, one of the first countries worldwide where the game became available, the release prompted internet providers to prepare for a spike in internet usage at midnight.

This is due to the game's size of 212 gigabytes, more than a quarter of the average New Zealand household's usage in a month.

Rockstar Games, the company behind the GTA franchise, faces high expectationsn after 13 years of anticipation, with previous versions having become commercial and cultural hits around the world.

GTA games sold 470 million units worldwide, and the fifth installment generated $1 billion in retail sales in just three days after its release in 2013.

Take-Two Interactive, Rockstar Games' parent company, saw its shares rise by more than eight percent the day before the game's release.

The game takes place in Vice City, a fictional version of Miami.

It follows Lucia Caminos and Jason Duval, a couple drawn into criminal networks while trying to secure a better life. GTA games are often criticized for including excessive violence and for glorifying crimes and drug use.

Lucia, a former prisoner, and Jason, an ex-military man, experience a series of criminal jobs that go wrong in the drug-trafficking world of Leonida, a fictional state based on Florida.

According to Rockstar, Leonida is the company's most detailed open world so far, extending beyond Vice City into smaller communities and nightlife hotspots inspired by locations in South Florida.

Music has been an intrinsic part of the GTA experience, and the latest game is expected to feature a soundtrack including artists like Tame Impala and SZA.

#134. Israel delays building contracts for West Bank settlement after UK sanctions

Attorney general says E1 settlement decision should not be taken by caretaker government led by Netanyahu

Israel has postponed issuing contracts for the construction of a large settlement on the West Bank seen as likely to render a two-state solution unviable, days after the UK imposed sanctions against building firms operating in the area.

Israeli diplomats insisted the move was not in response to the measures from Downing Street. Gali Baharav-Miara, the attorney general, said any decision on settlement expansion should not be taken by the caretaker government led by Benjamin Netanyahu but by whatever new administration was formed.

She ordered the Israel land authority and ministry of housing not to open the tender process. Israeli diplomats suggested the UK foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, had acted prematurely on Monday in sanctioning the eight construction firms. But UK diplomats believe the growing political pressure on Israel over settlements, including from US Democrats, is having an impact.

The deferral puts the E1 settlement expansion in the mix for any coalition negotiations. The leader of the Change bloc, Gadi Eisenkot, seen as Israel’s most likely next prime minister, has been clear in opposing violent settler activity, as has the newly elected Knesset speaker, Matan Kahana, an ally of Eisenkot.

Eisenkot has also opposed the E1 settlement expansion, describing it as a mistake likely to alienate international opinion. He said of the plan: “I recall that Netanyahu himself opposed this for a long time, and due to coalition pressure, he gave in and approved it.”

The EI settlement expansion, due to have been announced on Thursday, has long been seen as qualitatively different since the land – between Jerusalem and Ma’aleh Adumim, a West Bank settlement city of nearly 40,000 residents – bisects the West Bank and cuts it off from East Jerusalem.

Miliband has said it would mark the death knell of a future Palestinian state. The UK, along with a group of European countries, has recently recognised Palestine, and the preservation of a two-state solution has been described as the north star of Labour policy towards the region.

The construction tender was published for more than 3,401 housing units in the area. At the beginning of November, the Israel Defense Forces began demolishing homes in a Bedouin community within the E1 area.

Israel has already enforced the downgrading of the UK consulate in East Jerusalem as a reprisal for Miliband’s decision in September to try to end trade links with the illegal settlements.

Bennett said: “The British Labour government’s hostility, bias and one-sided approach are particularly glaring given its failure to take any action against the Palestinian Authority, which continues to encourage terrorism through financial payments, promote incitement in its education system and engage in illegal construction in violation of signed agreements.”

Although the Israeli election was largely fought on domestic security-related issues, Eisenkot seems to be more sensitive to the implications of Israel’s crumbling global reputation, and the need to make efforts to be seen to be listening to the criticism.

If he becomes prime minister, Eisenkot will, indirectly at least, also have to take account of the views of the Arab parties even if these parties are not going to become members of the government.

The group Peace Now welcomed the attorney general’s decision: “A government that lost the elections is not permitted to exploit its final days in power to advance destructive moves that will endanger Israel’s future and its international standing.

#132. Fiona the hippo undergoing care after swallowing a hat at Cincinnati Zoo

Watch: Fiona the hippo under observation after eating hat

The Cincinnati Zoo in Ohio says its beloved hippopotamus Fiona is undergoing emergency medical care after swallowing a knit hat that slipped into her enclosure.

The Zoo said that Fiona had swallowed the hat about nine days ago and was asymptomatic at first, adding that she was "large enough that a small-ish hat should be passing through".

But a few days later, her appetite started to decrease, and the zoo staff were trying to encourage Fiona to eat to help push the hat through her digestive system.

Fiona rose to fame when she was born premature at the Cincinnati Zoo and at first struggled to survive. Her story inspired an ice cream flavour as well as a character in Cincinnati's ballet.

Michelle Curley, a spokesperson for the zoo, told US media that the hat got into Fiona's enclosure by accident, after a visitor tossed it in the air and it caught on a ceiling fan that flung it through a small opening.

"We probably couldn't make [it] happen again if we tried," Curley said.

Zoo staff have been providing a series of supportive measures like fluids and ultrasounds to help Fiona recover.

The latest ultrasound showed the hat passing through Fiona's small intestine, the staff said, adding that she is still a bit dehydrated.

"Surgery isn't an option for full-grown hippos and our best chance for success with Fiona would be if she could pass this on her own," one of the zookeepers said in an update.

Fiona was born six weeks early to 17-year-old hippo Bibi on 24 January 2017.

At birth she weighed 13 kg (29 lbs), which the zoo said was about half the previous lowest recorded birth weight for her species.

The zoo said staff were "rooting for her and hoping that she proves to be as strong and resilient as she was at the beginning of her life".

#131. Almost 1 in 4 children live in conflict zones — report

Denial of humanitarian access, abductions and sexual violence are among several violations against children that have increased dramtically over the past year, according to a new report by Save the Children.

A record number of children were living in a conflict zone in 2025, Save the Children findings revealed on Thursday.

In the latest report in the "Stop the War on Children" series, the humantarian group said 589 million children worldwide lived in conflict zones. That's the highest number since records began in 1990, and more than double the total in the mid-1990s.

"For millions of children, fear, violence and displacement are part of every day life," said Save the Children Germany CEO Florian Westphal.

He said this needed to be a "wake-up call" for the international community.

Around two in five children in both Africa and the Middle East were living in conflict zones in 2025, the report found. Africa, alone, accounted for 44% of all children living in conflict zones worldwide.

In terms of regional proportions, 40.2% of children in the Middle East were living in conflict zones in 2025. That represented roughly two in five children across the region, and a particularly steep rise in the number of children affected. The figure jumped by nearly 29% from 52 million in 2024 to 67 million last year.

Children's exposure to conflict in Africa has more than tripled over the past 15 years, with the share of children living in conflict zones rising from 12% in 2010 to 39% in 2025.

The report highlighted the dramatic increase in the denial of humanitarian access.

A total of 8,322 verified incidents of denying access to humanitarian aid to children were recorded in 2025, a 16-fold rise over the past decade. The figure was 509 in 2015, and only eight incidents were reported when the United Nations (UN) established the Monitoring and Reporting Mechanism (MRM) in 2005.

The Palestinian Territories accounted for the vast majority of cases, with 5,946 incidents, or more than 70% of all verified cases globally.

"No grave violation has increased as dramatically or changed the overall pattern of verified violations as much as the denial of humanitarian access," the study noted.

According to the report, denial of humanitarian access rose from around 2% of all verified grave violations against children in 2015 to more than 21% in 2025, becoming the second-most frequently recorded violation after the killing and maiming of children.

Grave violations as laid out in the MRM also include the recruitment of children by armed forces, sexual violence, abduction and attacks on schools and hospitals.

The study found that, overall, 38,967 grave violations against children were verified by the UN.

This was the second-highest annual total since the MRM was established, surpassed only by the record 41,763 violations documented in 2024.

More than half of these took place in just three areas: the Palestinian Territories, the Democratic Republic of Congo and Nigeria. The Palestinian Territories alone accounted for 12,449 verified violations, nearly one-third of all cases.

As well as the denial of humanitarian access, violations in 2025 included:

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Palestinian Territories recorded the highest number of attacks on schools or hospitals, with 837 incidents, followed by Ukraine with 643. Together, the two accounted for more than half of all reported cases worldwide.

The sharpest rises in sexual violence incidents were recorded in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), where cases jumped from 358 in 2024 to 592 in 2025, and Sudan, which saw an increase from 74 to 193. Around 86% of all cases were documented in African conflict zones.

"Children have the right to live free from violence. Changing the odds requires political choices that put children’s safety first, and a sustained commitment to stop the war on children," the NGO said.

#129. Israeli tanks cross ‘yellow line’ boundary into Hamas-controlled central Gaza

First incursion across boundary since ceasefire deal comes as Eisenkot seeks to form government without Netanyahu

The Israeli military crossed into Hamas-controlled central Gaza on Wednesday in its first ground operation across the “yellow line” boundary since a fragile ceasefire was agreed last October, forcing dozens of displaced Palestinians to flee their tents.

Israeli tanks rolled along the Salah al-Din road, Gaza’s main north-south route, a day after the US-backed Board of Peace, a body overseeing the ceasefire, accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities.

An Israeli military spokesperson said troops were carrying out an operation targeting “Hamas infrastructure” west of the yellow line in central Gaza.

The military ​has not commented extensively on the operation, which came as the Israeli opposition politician, Gadi Eisenkot, has been seeking to form a government without the caretaker prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, who has built his political reputation on promises to keep Israel safe.

A Gaza security source told AFP there was “intense Israeli artillery shelling” north of Nuseirat refugee camp and reported Israeli military vehicles in central Gaza “amid ongoing gunfire and airstrikes since midday”.

Hamas condemned the Israeli raid and the forced displacement of Palestinians as “a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement” and a dangerous escalation. The Israeli military did not immediately respond to Hamas’s statement.

Residents reported hearing tank fire in the area. Palestinian medics said one person was killed in an Israeli airstrike near the site of the raid.

In separate incidents on Wednesday, Palestinian medics said two people were killed by Israeli fire, one in Deir al-Balah and the other in Gaza City. The Israeli military said it was unaware of any incident in Deir al-Balah and had struck a militant in Gaza City.

Israel has stepped up its attacks on Hamas, which it says has been regrouping and rearming. Hamas has accused Israel of trying to derail the US-brokered ceasefire agreed in October 2025.

Israeli strikes have continued since the ceasefire, and Hamas remains armed.

Last week, an Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in southern Gaza killed six people, including a surgeon and a disabled man, and wounded 28 others, according to the hospital director.

The body overseeing Donald Trump’s plan to end the war in Gaza has accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities, leaving Palestinians in dire conditions and the ceasefire at risk of collapse.

In its second report to the UN security council since the truce began 13 months ago, the Trump-led Board of Peace said the gap between what was agreed and what was happening on the ground remained the biggest threat to the deal. The report, seen by Reuters, warned that “a ceasefire that delivers nothing visible cannot be sustained indefinitely”.

Gaza’s health authorities say at least 1,600 Palestinians have been killed since the ceasefire came into effect. The Israeli military says five of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

Palestinian authorities say more than 74,000 people have been killed in Gaza since Israel launched its military campaign in response to the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, in which about 1,200 people were killed.

Reuters and AFP contributed to this report

#127. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#125. The leave campaign has been resurrected, so how should Burnham respond?

No 10 is confident the PM can make the case for closer EU ties, while others worry about leaving the campaign solely in his hands

The launch of a pro-Brexit campaign was one of the few genuine surprises this week in Westminster, even if the arguments were straight out of a familiar playbook.

The video launched on X over the weekend, with scenes of riots in France, champagne flowing in Brussels, and European politicians saying how desperate they were for Britain’s cash.

Among Westminster’s anti-Brexit veterans, there was immediate chatter about whether a counteroffensive was needed. “I think the immediate reaction was – are they off to the races while we are still putting our pants on?” one said.

For now, the rebuttal of Stay Out’s claims – including that returning to the EU would cost £750m a week – is mostly being done on social media by interested pro-rejoin freelancers, without any kind of central organisation.

One MP said: “It is hard for us to begin any kind of new pro-rejoin campaign until we know exactly the direction the government is heading.

“There are organisations that exist already, like Best for Britain, but I think making the case to the public would need a fresh movement. I think we want to stay away from the kind of marches that People’s Vote movement did with the blue berets if we can possibly avoid it.”

Inside No 10, there is a confidence that Andy Burnham can make his own case for a closer relationship with Europe, without the need for any external pressure group. Stay Out – which is run by the former deputy chief Brexit negotiator Oliver Lewis – will be dubbed “the status quo campaign”.

There is reason to be confident. Strategists have seen Labour polling that shows almost three-quarters – 71% – of all voters support a closer relationship with Europe if it helps bring down prices and grow the economy.

It will be the prime minister, they say, who can front any campaigning that is needed, who can argue that Brexit has done more harm than good, damaging the economy, making it harder for British people to travel and businesses to trade and fuelling the small boats crisis.

However, there will be some fears that this has been tried before when David Cameron attempted to make the case for remain in 2016. But Labour strategists say this is a fundamentally different political moment. “Cameron could not make this kind of emotional connection with people, it was business leaders, facts and figures, and he was arguing for the status quo,” one said. “We are the change campaign now.”

While some cabinet members urged caution last week over Burnham’s uncertain direction on Europe, most trust the prime minister can set out a viable post-summit plan. “All of us, honestly, are slightly running to catch up with a different way of doing things,” one said. “We all easily lapse into the worries about leave-voting seats.

“But there is obviously an argument to be made that the old rules don’t apply. And Andy’s way of doing things is laying out political argument. And we have to believe in that as the way to affect public sentiment.”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

There are some close to Labour who worry about leaving the argument solely to Burnham. “Can they do this from the bowels of Downing Street?” one asked. “You do need rebuttal out there and people ready to go out and fight and say, this is bollocks. And it felt like that infrastructure is not there yet.”

Another veteran of the remain campaign said there was concern about being “left out on a limb” if a rejoin campaign was resurrected. “I think whether they like it or not, there will be an apolitical campaign trying to push the maximalist position on Europe. They have people willing to help but it’s not organised in any way.”

Many of those who worked on the remain campaign and the subsequent People’s Vote are scarred by the experience, particular the final months which descended into bitter infighting. But the same is also true for some of the most high-profile veterans of Vote Leave – who also want to steer clear of Stay Out.

As in the pro-Brexit campaigns, there are often willing funders to push pro-European campaigning. “It will happen because there will be money for it,” one campaign veteran said. “But you could not pay me any money to go back to that issue.”

Follow the latest on Thursday, 19th November, below:

The Crimean Bridge was attacked for a third time this week, a Russian official said on Thursday.

The UK pledged to provide Ukraine with long-range cruise missiles capable of reaching areas in Russia beyond the front lines.

#121. EU ‘to close door’ to talks on long-term UK ties until Burnham has decided on Brexit

Diplomats say piecemeal approach of resets is coming to an end and Europe wants clarity on whether Britain wants to reverse its exit

The EU will shut the door to discussions with Andy Burnham about the UK’s longer-term relationship with the bloc until the government has decided whether it wants to proceed with reversing Brexit, diplomats have said.

European capitals are also warning they have reached the end of the line for piecemeal “reset” summits with the UK and want the prime minister to focus on his next steps domestically before they proceed.

But they suggested a joint statement after a summit this Friday would signal that further discussions would follow on the UK’s entry to the EU’s energy single markets, irregular migration and a closer defence and security relationship.

“Clarity is what we need more than anything from the UK. Don’t come back until you know what you want. Work out what your options really are and then come to negotiate,” one EU diplomat said.

“The prime minister needs to bring the public with him. We can’t have another negotiation based on just half the country. We’ll only put the effort in if we know it’s not going to change down the line.”

Burnham is expected to set out his own next steps after the summit on Monday, including his hopes for a “national conversation” on what a closer relationship might look like.

With less than 48 hours before the Brussels summit, negotiators were still racing to finalise Friday’s agreement. A leaked draft seen by the Guardian shows continued disagreement over demands by the EU that the UK commit to aligning with the bloc’s trade defence measures, such as tariffs on Chinese electric vehicles, in exchange for inclusion in Made in Europe legislation.

The text shows the UK is pushing for a weaker commitment, only promising an “aim to protect supply chains consistently across our markets and guard against potential risks of circumvention”.

A Cabinet Office source said Made in Europe language was “the highest jeopardy” in the negotiations, while an EU official suggested it was the part of the discussions most likely to go “down to the wire” on Friday.

Though there is a formal commitment to annual reset summits, senior EU diplomats say the current approach, designed by Keir Starmer, will quickly “reach its limits” and more “mini deals” do not make sense.

One said the “piecemeal approach is coming to an end” and that Burnham’s intentions for the UK’s long-term relationship created a natural juncture for the EU too.

Another said: “It is like a buffet: you can choose to nibble on one or two dishes to start, but at a certain point you have to go for the whole of the buffet.”

One EU diplomat said there would be a “pause for reflection” after the Friday summit.

Burnham has said there is no rush to decide on the longer-term relationship, as he comes under pressure from within his own party to slow the pace of pursuing of closer ties.

He is not now expected to set out the full range of options for the future relationship – the status quo, customs union, single market or full rejoin – until he announces his 10-year plan in December.

Sources said the government was considering holding a year-long “national conversation” in 2027, including town halls and citizens assemblies, before deciding which route to pursue in advance of the next election.

However, the potential length of that debate has alarmed Brussels officials, who were caught off-guard by the launch of the new pro-Brexit campaign group Stay Out, set up by architects of the Vote Leave campaign.

One EU diplomat said: “The government would spend the year making evidence-based arguments about each of the four options, while Boris Johnson and Michael Gove and Nigel Farage will spend it shouting about traitors.”

After the reset deal is agreed on Friday, it will be enacted by the European partnership bill, covering the deals on food and agriculture, emissions trading and youth mobility. It is expected to be introduced before Christmas, with legislation laid possibly as soon as next week.

EU diplomats said the two sides were expected to settle on a quota of about 100,000 people a year being allowed to work, travel or study in each other’s territories. Originally, the UK had pitched for 50,000 and then 80,000, while Brussels has consistently sought a higher number.

Both sides have agreed that tuition fees for European students coming to the UK should be discussed at a later date, although there have been attempts by some member states to link the debate to the ratification of the whole reset deal.

Senior Labour figures are already beginning their push to have a full rejoining on the table. The former business secretary Peter Kyle said Burnham should fight the next election on a promise to rejoin the EU, with voters given a referendum on the outcome of any deal.

Kyle, who was a key member of Starmer’s cabinet, said the “only way through” the challenges of the domestic political debate was for the government to offer voters the choice to accept or reject any agreement.

He is the latest senior Labour MP to publicly wade into the Brexit debate, after the former foreign secretary David Lammy said the party should go into the next election clear that its intention was to rejoin.

Rachel Reeves, the former chancellor, has also said Britain’s future should be back in the bloc, arguing that leaving had made the UK “poorer, weaker and less secure”.

#119. Why has Waymo pulled all its driverless cars from US freeways?

Waymo has temporarily suspended driverless rides on US freeways after California banned the service over safety concerns.

California's Department of Motor Vehicles said a series of traffic incidents, including robotaxis entering active construction zones, posed an imminent risk to public safety. Waymo has now restricted freeway operations in all other US markets while it reviews the performance of its vehicles.

Waymo currently operates in 15 US cities, though only offered driverless freeway rides in a handful of cities, including San Francisco and Los Angeles.

#118. Crimean Bridge has been attacked again, Russian official says

The Crimean Bridge has been attacked again, Crimea's Russian-installed governor said. The attack comes as Vladimir Putin prepares to sit down with Donald Trump and Xi Jinping for joint talks in China.

The Crimean Bridge was attacked for a third time this week, a Russian official said on Thursday.

Traffic had been temporarily suspended, according to Crimea's Russian-installed governor.

"The damage is currently being assessed," Sergei Aksyonov, the governor, said on Telegram, adding that emergency services were at the scene.

The bridge, which supports a four-lane roadway and a railroad, is the only bridge linking mainland Russia with occupied Crimea.

This is the third Ukrainian attack this week targeting the strategic bridge that is a key symbol of Russia's annexation of Crimea in 2014.

The bridge has played a key role in supplying Russian troops in occupied parts of southern Ukraine since the 2022 Russian invasion, making it a key target for Kyiv.

On Tuesday, Ukraine carried out a major attack on the bridge using missiles and drones, destroying the steel arch of the 19-kilometer-long road section of the crossing.

Ukraine's SBU security service had said that the "road span beneath it (the arch) has been completely destroyed."

The latest attack appears to have impacted rail traffic on the bridge, according to German press agency DPA.

The Ukrainian attack came just hours before an unprecedented meeting scheduled between Putin and his US and Chinese counterparts on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) summit.
