#86. Bancroft sandpaper claims heap pressure on Cummins

Cameron Bancroft and Steve Smith were banned for their part in the sandpaper plot

Cameron Bancroft has claimed the whole Australia team knew about the 2018 sandpaper plot in explosive revelations that will heap pressure on current captain Pat Cummins.

In a detailed testimony, published by the West Australian,, external batter Bancroft - the player caught with the sandpaper during the Test match against South Africa - has given arguably the most complete account of the events in Cape Town eight years ago.

"In 2018 not the entire truth was told," said Bancroft. "I have never been completely honest about what happened."

For the past two weeks, Australian cricket has been embroiled in a debate over the ball-tampering saga following the publication of Usman Khawaja's autobiography.

Khawaja wrote that the whole Australia team knew of the plot, claims supported by David Warner and Chadd Sayers. Cummins described Khawaja's account as "wrong" and "nonsense".

On the day Cricket Australia announced a forum into the sandpaper episode, Bancroft published his account, explaining he was motivated by the news conference given by Cummins.

"The final straw for me to write this account was Australian cricket captain Pat Cummins' press conference," said Bancroft.

"It made me feel angry, betrayed and alone.

"What I saw, experienced and lived with - not only him, but everyone else on that tour in South Africa - is not the public account he gave in his press conference and in the years previously when he has spoken on this time."

The sandpaper plot - who was there, who said what?

Opener Bancroft was given a nine-month ban for his part in the plot.

Steve Smith was stripped of the Australia captaincy and given a year-long ban, the same suspension given to vice-captain Warner. No other players or coaches were punished.

In his more than 4,000-word piece, Bancroft has claimed:

Sandpaper was first used during the second Test against South Africa â the match before Bancroft was caught

Bancroft had conversations with the Australia seamers â Cummins, Mitchell Starc, Josh Hazlewood and Mitchell Marsh about whether the ball was "swinging enough"

Before the third day of the second Test, the entire playing group and staff had "celebrations" about the effect of the sandpaper

When Bancroft was caught during the third Test, then Australia coach Darren Lehmann told 12th man Peter Handscomb to "get the sandpaper off Bangers now"

Bancroft found the publication of a joint-statement by the Australia bowlers in 2021 "deeply painful".

Khawaja's autobiography and the subsequent claims made by Warner and seamer Sayers have reopened old wounds related to the sandpaper saga.

Still, those connected have remained true to the accounts they have presented during the intervening eight years.

The most significant denial came from Cummins, who rebuffed Khawaja's account in an extraordinary 68-minute address to the media in Sydney. Lehmann and Starc have also reiterated their denials.

Now, it is the extensive detail provided by Bancroft that could shed a whole new light on the controversy and lead to further questions for Cummins, Lehmann and Cricket Australia.

Cummins is due to lead Australia in the first Test against New Zealand on 9 December.

A statement released on behalf of Cummins said: "Whilst we feel for Cam and everything he's been through there's nothing in the statement that hasn't been answered by Pat in his press conference."

A Cricket Australia statement said: "We respect that Cameron Bancroft has told his story, and we will take time to consider his comments.

"We expect that there will be many dimensions to the events of 2018, which is why we have established the forum for individuals to give their version of events.

"We are finalising the terms of reference and will commence the process as soon as possible."

In his account, Bancroft said: "I have spent eight years speaking about accountability, honesty and the lessons I learned from one of Australian cricket's darkest moments.

"However, in 2018 not the entire truth was told. I have never been completely honest about what happened.

"It was not just the three of us â Steve Smith, David Warner and I â who knew about me using sandpaper on the ball in South Africa. It was the whole team.

"When I was questioned during Cricket Australia's investigation, I did not give a complete and truthful account.

"I withheld important details, including when the use of sandpaper began, how long it went on for, and the involvement and awareness of others.

"I did this to protect Cricket Australia's investigation and not implicate others around me."

'No way in a million years' the whole team didn't know - Vaughan on sandpaper scandal

Bancroft said his actions were "discussed" and "acknowledged" by the entire playing group at the end of the second day and before the beginning of the third day. He said he was encouraged by Saker, who has since worked as a consultant with England.

The only person within the camp to express unease at the ball-tampering, according to Bancroft, was media manager Kate Hutchison.

"I received wraps and encouragement from members of the playing and coaching staff, especially David Saker," said Bancroft.

"Our media manager, Kate Hutchison, expressed her objection to what was happening.

"I recall her making it clear that she believed it was wrong, that she wanted nothing to do with it, and walking away from the discussion.

"I will never forget that. Somebody had spoken up.

"Somebody had demonstrated that being part of the team did not mean having to accept everything the team was doing. And I ignored it."

Bancroft identifies Saker as the coach Khawaja claimed encouraged him to work harder on the ball during the third Test in Cape Town. The 33-year-old said he took "feedback" from all the bowlers and wicketkeeper Tim Paine, who took over the captaincy when Smith was sacked.

In Cape Town, Bancroft was confronted by the umpires when TV cameras showed him concealing the sandpaper in his trousers. He claimed Lehmann sent a message to him via Handscomb.

"Peter Handscomb ran out at the drinks break after I'd been caught and shoved the sandpaper down my pants," wrote Bancroft.

"He said to me: 'I think it's time I take that off you now.' And Pete was doing his job after Boof [Lehmanm] shouted to him on the walkie talkie "get the sandpaper off Bangers [Bancroft] now".

"If he didn't know he wouldn't have said that. And if it wasn't clear enough before, then it was obvious now."

In 2021, Bancroft was playing county cricket with Durham and gave an interview to The Guardian in which he said the bowlers' involvement in the plot was "self-explantory".

In response, Cummins, Starc, Hazlewood and spinner Nathan Lyon released a joint-statement denying any knowledge that ball-tampering had taken place.

"Reading that statement was deeply painful," said Bancroft.

"I had memories of conversations on the field in Port Elizabeth and Newlands, feedback about the ball and shared celebrations that made the statement difficult for me to digest."

'Cricket Australia didn't get it right' - Gillespie

For almost two weeks, Australian cricket has been at war with itself over the revelations in Usman Khawaja's autobiography.

But there has been an absence of a smoking gun. Without tangible, new, hard evidence, all that was left was claim and counter-claim.

Naturally, the spotlight fell on Pat Cummins because of his role as a bowler in Cape Town and his elevation to Australia captain in the years since.

His performance last week in front of the media, on live television, was extraordinary. For 68 minutes he took all questions and appeared plausible in his answers.

In being so robust in his denial of Khawaja's account - Cummins called it "nonsense" - the skipper also left himself open to the consequences if it was later revealed he was not telling the truth.

Crucially, it was Cummins' press conference that compelled Cameron Bancroft to speak out.

It must be stressed that Bancroft's account is not the open-and-shut evidence that convicts Cummins or anyone else in the dressing room at Newlands in 2018.

But it is certainly the most complete account we have heard from anyone involved, walking step-by-step through the genesis of the plot, the execution and the fallout. Bancroft has named names: Cummins, the other bowlers, Tim Paine, coach Darren Lehmann and assistant coach David Saker. It is, potentially, the decisive intervention that shifts this whole saga in a new direction.

Whether by accident or design, it came on the day Cricket Australia (CA) announced its forum for evidence into the sandpaper controversy. Before Bancroft's account CA's action felt an unsatisfactory halfway house - not an investigation, with no guarantee outcomes will be published.

What now? Will CA go further? How will those implicated by Bancroft respond? How can Cummins lead Australia in the first Test against New Zealand? Could Steve Smith break a silence he has held since the publication of Khawaja's book?

Throughout the past fortnight, there has been a sense that Australian cricket hoped the sandpaper debate would eventually go away. Cummins even said he never wanted to speak about it again.

Instead of fading, the thirst for the truth grows, now fuelled by Bancroft's extraordinary story.

#84. Hi-tech Shenzhen takes centre stage as Xi sends ‘Chinamaxxing’ message at Apec summit

Host city offers showcase of China’s rise into a confident and innovative power central to global economy

In a Shenzhen square on a balmy evening, locals and tourists watched the dozens of glinting towers that make up the city’s skyline ripple through the colour spectrum – cyan and violet, cyber green, China’s national scarlet and gold – repeatedly flashing the words “Apec 2026”.

The Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit is a dream showcase for one of China’s most advanced cities, and the country’s leader, Xi Jinping, is basking in its neon glow.

On the sidelines, a series of high-profile bilateral meetings and the announcement of a historic three-way summit with Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin have cemented Xi’s status as an agenda setter on the global stage.

The broader Apec summit, meanwhile, provides an opportunity for China to present itself as a steward of multilateralism against what it terms “unilateralism and protectionism” – that is to say, trade measures taken against Beijing by countries such as the US.

All this is set against the futuristic backdrop that is Shenzhen, a city that perhaps better than no other embodies China’s rise into a confident, technologically innovative power central to the global economy.

A photo display in the Apec media centre charts Shenzhen’s transformation from sparsely populated marshland, calling it “a miracle in the history of world development created by the Chinese people”.

“[Xi] wants people to go home Chinamaxxing,” said Prof Astrid Nordin, of King’s College London. “Shenzhen is itself part of the message. It’s a showcase of Chinese technological and economic achievements, and of China’s claim that it offers opportunities for prosperity in a world increasingly characterised by uncertainty and conflict.”

Like the light show, the summit is being carefully coordinated. The Financial Times cited people familiar with the matter as saying it would be one of the most tightly controlled in the organisation’s history, with leaders having to travel to a state guesthouse beside Shenzhen’s tiger safari park to meet Xi. The paper said diplomats had grumbled that the format “seems designed to reduce the free-flowing discussion characteristic of prior editions”.

Setting out its expectations for the summit, China’s foreign ministry has said it looks forward to “practical results in key areas such as trade, connectivity, digital economy, AI and so on”.

Already, Xi has announced trade wins with Canada’s Mark Carney, extending remission measures for certain Chinese steel and aluminium products by two years in exchange for tariff relief on Canadian pork, fish and seafood exports.

A meeting with Papua New ⁠Guinea’s leader, meanwhile, showed how Beijing’s geopolitical priorities have been absorbed and reflected by its allies, with James Marape recounting to Xi how he had closed Taiwan’s office in his country despite domestic challenges in doing so.

The most important meeting will be between Xi and Trump. The leaders of the world’s two superpowers are expected to tackle a broad range of issues including AI and trade. In a move that could strain the fragile tariff truce that the two leaders first agreed last year, the Trump administration said on Monday it would further restrict China’s access to advanced AI chips.

Christopher Adams, a senior adviser at the law firm Covington who was at the centre of trade negotiations with China for the Obama and first Trump administrations, said: “The trade truce may increasingly look like a ceasefire in a war where major combat operations remain on hold but skirmishes are escalating to the point where the line between peace and war is blurred.

“I don’t see pressure to announce a further extension of the trade truce in Shenzhen. If a significant extension is announced, that would be an upside surprise.”

China has not hesitated to leverage Apec’s multinational platform in service of its arguments. A commentary by the state news agency Xinhua on Tuesday said: “Amid deepening geopolitical rivalries and growing global divisions, [China’s hosting] sends a clear signal that great-power competition need not be a zero-sum game, and that one country’s development need not come at the expense of another.”

The chair of the Apec senior officials’ meeting, Chen Xu, said Beijing had made trade “openness” a key focus of its Apec year and looked forward to “achieving more practical outcomes on the trade front”.

The much anticipated trilateral summit with Trump and Putin, on the other hand, could prove largely symbolic. For Xi, “it provides the appearance of China shaping the world order as an equal to the United States,” said Dr Kevork Oskanian, an expert on Russia at the University of Exeter. “Agreement on substantial issues, which would, in any case, require extensive preparatory negotiations, is unlikely.”

For China’s ruling Communist party, the political pomp has another key purpose. Nordin said summits were “occasions for leaders to tell stories about the world, about their country’s place in it … and one of the most important audiences for those stories is at home.”

For the locals watching the skyscraper light show on Monday night, the symbolism was not lost. China’s export might had helped it become increasingly powerful on the world stage, said one local man, who did not give his name. “It’s so great that the summit will take place in Shenzhen,” he said. “I feel very proud.”

#80. Democrats win Alaska Senate race two weeks after midterms counting began

Mary Peltola defeated the state's Republican incumbent after a controversial race

Democrat Mary Peltola has won Alaska's hard-fought Senate race, completing a sweeping victory for the party in the US upper house in the midterm elections.

The win gives the Democrats a 56th Senate seat following a tumultuous and controversial election in Alaska, which featured alleged spoiler recruitment efforts and unprecedented court battles.

It took two full weeks of counting to call a winner in the 3 November poll due to the logistical challenge of collecting ballots from across the state's remote expanse.

It is the ninth Senate seat the Democrats have taken from Republicans in the midterms, giving the party control of the US Congress for the last two years of Donald Trump's presidency.

The Democrats also won back the House of Representatives, the lower chamber.

Peltola defeated the incumbent Republican senator, Dan S Sullivan, who is yet to comment.

In her victory statement, Peltola said the "status quo in DC is not working for Alaska".

She recalled speaking to "elders in tears about empty fish racks" and to parents in rural Alaska choosing between heating their homes and putting food on the table.

Peltola is the first Alaska Native elected to the US Senate, and is the first Democrat to win a US Senate seat in Alaska since 2008.

Donald Trump won the state by 13 points in the 2024 presidential election, but defeat for the Republicans in this year's US Senate race in the state caps off a string of losses in the midterms for the president's party.

Many voters across the United States cited cost-of-living issues as a factor that affected how they cast their ballots.

Trump says Alaska voters chose the wrong Dan Sullivan. What does the data show?

Could Alaska's fishing industry tilt Washington's balance of power?

Peltola's campaign had come under fire after the New York Times, external reported that her team worked with people who tried to recruit so-called spoiler candidates in an alleged attempt to confuse voters.

The alleged scheme led to a candidate named Daniel J Sullivan - a complete political unknown residing in a small coastal town who shared a name with the state's current US Senator and incumbent, Dan S Sullivan - contesting the race.

After legal challenges, state courts ruled that Daniel J Sullivan could remain on the ballot, and election officials distinguished them on election materials.

Peltola denied having knowledge of the alleged plot but her campaign swiftly came under fire, and both the US justice department and Alaska's Republican governor said they would launch investigations.

The Trump administration also filed a lawsuit attempting to stop the certification of the election results if the incumbent Republican Senator Sullivan lost.

A federal judge, though, dismissed the case, following legal challenges from Alaska election officials as well as the Peltola campaign and voting groups.

Due to Alaska's ranked-choice voting system, voters confused about which Dan Sullivan was the incumbent could have simply chosen both men, listing them as their first and second choices.

If they did so, after the first round of voting was complete, tabulation would have re-allocated any votes from Daniel J Sullivan - who stood little to no chance of winning - to Dan S Sullivan.

Experts told the BBC that this process made it difficult for any potential spoiler candidate to have any impact on the results.

An analysis by BBC Verify found that even if every vote for Daniel J Sullivan had gone to the incumbent, Dan S Sullivan, Peltola would still have won.

The Democratic win in Alaska cements the party's majority in the US Senate, as the party now has 56 seats to the Republicans' 44.

The most senior Democrat in the Senate, Chuck Schumer, said Peltola had run "a pitch-perfect campaign devoted to the issues Alaskans care most about". Hailing the Democrats' final tally in the Senate, he said: "56 seats was an unimaginable result even just a few months ago."

The Alaska seat was initially one of several toss-up races that appeared as a must-win for Democrats if they wanted to gain control of the US Senate. But by the time Peltola was called the winner, they had seats to spare.

With 99% of ballots counted, Peltola had slightly more than 50% of the votes, while Sullivan trailed on less than 46%.

Sullivan, a former Alaska attorney general and an ally of Trump, campaigned on his record of delivering energy and military investment for the state.

Peltola promised Alaskans a more independent voice in Washington.

"As a lifelong Alaskan who has dedicated my life to serving our state, there is no greater honor than having Alaskans' support in our fight to take on corporate greed, lower costs, and put Alaska first," she said in her victory statement.

"I will stand up to anyone to take on the rigged system in DC and deliver results for Alaska's working families."

#79. Jensen Huang to testify before Congress on ‘potential breach of national security’

High-stakes hearing comes in wake of resignation from Nvidia after alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia’s former CEO Jensen Huang will testify before several congressional committees on 1 December, setting the stage for a high-stakes hearing in the wake of his resignation following an affair with a suspected Chinese spy.

Tim Teter, Nvidia’s general counsel, has also agreed to appear for a closed-door transcribed interview the following day, 2 December.

“We look forward to hearing from Jensen Huang and Tim Teter on their roles in a potential breach of our national security,” Congressmen Brian Mast, John Moolenaar and Rick Crawford, who chair the committees, said in a press release. “The American people need answers on Mr Huang and Nvidia’s role in allowing dangerous [People’s Republic of China] actors to access our most advanced chips, as well as the role of a potential Chinese intelligence asset in this scandal.”

The three congressmen chair the foreign affairs committee, the select committee on the Chinese Communist party, and the intelligence committee of the House of Representatives, respectively. On 12 November, they had signed a letter warning Huang that he could face a subpoena if he did not agree to the closed-door meeting.

Nvidia chips are key to powering the artificial intelligence boom. The Trump administration has prohibited the selling of advanced artificial intelligence chips to China.

Huang stepped down as Nvidia’s CEO after allegations of extramarital relationships with the suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang and two Nvidia employees surfaced.

Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, had been Megaspeed International’s executive director. The Singapore-based cloud computing company has bought nearly $5bn in AI chips and advanced hardware. The company said Alice Huang stepped down in 2025.

Megaspeed ultimately facilitated about $12bn in chip sales, in violation of US export controls. On Friday, Malaysia stated that they were investigating whether the company diverted advanced AI ships to other countries.

A recent joint Department of Commerce and Department of Justice letter, stated that the Department of Commerce, under its secretary, Howard Lutnick, first started an investigation in Megaspeed on 2024 for potential export control violations. The justice department, led by the attorney general, Todd Blanche, took over in 2025.

In his first remarks since his resignation, Jensen Huang said on Thursday he would be willing to return to Nvidia as CEO. Elon Musk and other Silicon Valley tech giants, along with 165,000 signatories to an open letter, have called for his reinstatement. Trump has also signaled his openness for the disgraced tech CEO’s return.

#78. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#74. Julio Iglesias facing court investigation over alleged sexual offences

Singer Julio Iglesias is facing an investigation by Spain's national court over allegations he sexually abused and assaulted two female former staff members

Two women, who worked for the 83-year-old in the Caribbean, have accused him of abuse which they say took place in the Bahamas and the Dominican Republic in 2021.

The announcement comes after Spanish prosecutors dropped an inquiry into the claims earlier this year after concluding that they lacked jurisdiction because the alleged crimes took place abroad.

Iglesias, who has been a household name in Spain since the 1960s and has sold millions of records worldwide, has previously denied the allegations.

But in a statement published on Wednesday, external (in Spanish), national court Judge Antonio PiÃ±a said the account given in the complaint, alongside preliminary evidence provided, justified opening criminal proceedings.

He added that the investigation would cover parts of the complaint relating to potential crimes against sexual freedom, as well as assault and degrading treatment.

But the court will not investigate claims made in the lawsuit of human trafficking or crimes against workers' rights. PiÃ±a said these allegations were dismissed after he looked into the hiring of the women and found there was "no deception or artifice involved".

PiÃ±a said the former employees had been summoned to testify in person on 3 February.

The announcement comes after Spain's public prosecutor's office shifted its position last month, saying it now believed the court had jurisdiction to investigate the allegations.

In its statement, the national court said Spanish law sets out that courts can hear cases of crimes committed abroad by Spanish citizens provided certain conditions are met.

The allegations against Iglesias first emerged in a joint three-year investigation between Spanish newspaper El Diario, external and television network Univision news, published in January.

In that investigation, the two women were identified as a domestic worker and a physical therapist, who described alleged sexual abuse and degrading working conditions.

The domestic worker, whose name was changed to Rebeca in the reports, said Iglesias would regularly call her to his room at the end of the day and would touch her inappropriately without consent.

Rebeca, a Dominican who was 22 when the alleged events took place, also alleged that Iglesias had forced her to take part in threesomes with another female member of staff. She also described the singer slapping her in the face and grabbing her genitals.

The other woman, a Venezuelan physical therapist named as Laura in the article, said Iglesias had touched her breasts and kissed her on the mouth against her will. She added that he constantly threatened to fire her, controlled how much food she ate and asked her about when her period was due.

Rebeca and Laura filed an initial legal complaint in January against Iglesias for sexual assault and human trafficking.

In a response to the allegations in January, Iglesias said, external: "I deny having abused, coerced, or disrespected any woman. These accusations are absolutely false and deeply sadden me."

On Wednesday, the court said the lawsuit had been accepted based on evidence provided in the complaint. This included a photograph of one of the complainants, showing a bruise on her arm that was "consistent with the possibility of her being grabbed forcefully".

It added that the same photo showed "two marks on one of her thighs that could correspond to a human bite", which the complainant said were "a consequence of her forced participation in acts of a sexual nature".

WhatsApp message exchanges, including New Year's greetings, which suggest an "affectionate" relationship between the women and Iglesias, will also be considered, the court said.

But it added that the seemingly cordial tone, which presents "an apparent discrepancy with certain aspects of the narrative", would be clarified through a "complete and contextualised examination of the communications".

The claim over violations of workers' rights could not be investigated because there is no comparable criminal offence in the Bahamas and the Dominican Republic, where the alleged crimes took place, it said.

Iglesias is one of the most successful Latin artists of all time, having sold more than 300 million records in a career spanning decades.

He is also the father of fellow music superstar Enrique Iglesias.

#70. South Korea’s high-stakes university exams upended by AI cheating scandal

South Korea has been gripped by revelations of AI-aided cheating, just days before more than half a million students are to sit high-stakes entrance exams

The integrity of South Korea’s career-defining university entrance exam has been thrown into doubt after the top scorer in a recent professional exam admitted using a concealed camera and AI to cheat.

On Thursday, more than half a million people, mostly school students, are due to sit the exam, known as the suneung, at 1,312 centres across the country. The exam runs for a gruelling eight hours over a single day and determines which university a candidate can attend and, in turn, their future prospects.

The country goes to great lengths to ensure the smooth running of the suneung. Aircraft take-offs and landings are rescheduled during the 25-minute English listening paper to minimise noise, while artillery fire and tank movements are suspended.

The stock market opens an hour late and cars are stopped 200 metres from every centre. Extra trains run from 6am, police drive latecomers to their exam halls, and tourist attractions open late to ease traffic. Offices are asked to open at 10am.

But in recent days, exam organisers have faced a renewed threat to the validity of the entire exercise, after the top scorer in this year’s tax accountancy exam- who got just one question wrong out of 160– admitted on Sunday he had cheated by using AI.

Prime minister Han Seong-sook told a cabinet meeting in Seoul on Tuesday that AI-assisted cheating was a “social evil” that the government would “root out”.

“We will block outright the carrying of smart devices during the exam,” she said. The education ministry and other agencies should approach it “as if staking their jobs on it”, she added.

Han said smart devices must be turned off and handed in, while bags left in corridors would be checked for any devices that were still on. Exam officials would also inspect all candidates’ glasses and watches, as well as their collars, sleeves and buttons for concealed cameras.

The government will also send emergency alerts to every phone in the country, which sound at full volume and cannot be silenced, in the hope of catching out anyone who has not handed in their device.

More invigilators will be deployed, and the government said it was preparing to switch off school wifi inside exam centres and public wifi nearby, and that personal hotspots, which cannot be blocked, would be traced through connection records.

The weekend’s cheating revelation captured national headlines in South Korea, stoking concern about the security of the suneung.

In a video posted to his lawyer’s YouTube channel, the exam cheat said he had photographed the questions with a concealed microcamera, sent them to an AI system and read the answers from a smartphone. “I cheated in the tax accountant exam,” he said. “My top placing is not the result of legitimate effort.”

His lawyer handed police a camera, a laptop and a phone. No charges have been brought, and the prime minister has ordered procedures to begin that would void his result and bar him for five years.

The education minister, Choi Kyo-jin, told parliament on Monday that while authorities could screen out the smart glasses already on sale at Thursday’s exam, newer variants could not be dealt with perfectly.

Choi has ruled out postponing the exam, which has been delayed several times before, including in 2017, after an earthquake struck the south-eastern city of Pohang, and in 2020 due to the Covid pandemic.

Smart glasses have been banned in exams since the end of June. In October, several candidates were caught with them while sitting university-run admissions exams. At the suneung, wearing a pair counts as using one even when switched off.

The education ministry looked at using radio-frequency equipment to detect the glasses in exam halls, but concluded conditions varied too much between venues for deployment this year. Checks will instead rely on visual inspection, with at least one dedicated official placed in each exam centre.

Teachers’ unions have criticised the government response as belated. A survey last month of 2,716 secondary school teachers by the Korean Teachers and Education Workers Union showed that 82.3% said they could not tell whether a candidate was wearing smart glasses under the current procedure, with only 3.5% saying they could.

To avoid facing scrutiny on exam day, some candidates are reportedly switching to contact lenses. An optician in Suwon, south of Seoul, said students who had never worn contacts were coming in to ask how to put them in.

Han said on X on Monday that the checks would be handled carefully “so that candidates acting in good faith do not suffer unnecessary anxiety or inconvenience”.

In his video confession, the accountancy test cheat addressed suneung candidates directly. “The suneung is coming up soon,” he said.

“To anyone thinking of cheating as I did: I am going to tell the investigators everything, so please don’t think, ‘I won’t get caught.’”

#69. Democrats nominate Hakeem Jeffries to be US Speaker of the House

Democrats in the US House of Representatives officially nominated Hakeem Jeffries to be Speaker, setting him on the path to become the first black lawmaker to lead the chamber.

The House will elect its new Speaker on 3 January, when the next term begins and Democrats will be in the majority.

His election to the powerful position - the Speaker is in line to succeed the president after the vice-president - is widely expected. Should he win, Jeffries will preside over his party's largest House majority in years and benefit from the Senate also being under Democrats' control.

Jeffries told reporters after the vote that the nomination was "a high honour and a distinct privilege".

As of Wednesday, Democrats will hold at least 257 seats in the House next year, which is well beyond the 218 needed for a majority. Seven races from the recent midterm elections are still uncalled.

Jeffries, who represents a district in New York, was re-elected as the party leader in a 251-11 closed-door vote on Wednesday. He has served as minority leader since 2023.

The Congressional Black Caucus, currently made up of only Democratic House members, noted the potential history-making moment when Jeffries takes the helm of the House.

"The American people, largely driven by turnout among Black communities, particularly across the south, have trusted us to make Washington work for them," said the committee's chair, Yvette Clarke, in a statement. "Now, it is our charge and responsibility to do all we can to deliver on their faith."

Despite overwhelming support in Wednesday's vote , Jeffries faced a handful of holdouts from new members in the party's left wing, who have said the party's sweeping victory in the midterm elections is a sign voters want change.

"While we have seen meaningful progress, there is still work to be done to ensure that the direction our party takes in this coming Congress reflects what our constituents are demanding of our leadership," seven incoming lawmakers, including fellow New Yorkers Darializa Avila Chevalier and Claire Valdez, said in a statement.

All congressional Democrats are gearing up for clashes with the White House once they officially take control in January.

President Donald Trump, a Republican, has signalled a willingness to work with Democrats on some issues, but has also pledged to use his veto power to reject legislation he does not like.

Trump's opposition could make it hard for Democrats to deliver on their campaign promises to address affordability and pass other reforms.

At a news conference following the vote on Wednesday, Jeffries pledged that House Democrats would "fight to lower the high costs of groceries and gas and housing", and "make healthcare affordable for every single American".

Though the party will have a large enough majority to weather defections on must-pass legislation, Jeffries will still have to navigate internal ideological divisions that have grown particularly sharp over US support of Israel and Americans' rising healthcare costs.

Many incoming Democrats, including members of the Democratic Socialists of America, won their elections by challenging the party establishment.

The Congressional Progressive Caucus, from the party's left wing, congratulated Jeffries on the nomination, adding they would "continue conversations with Leader Jeffries to ensure Democrats deliver a bold agenda for working people".

Along with shepherding legislation, Jeffries as Speaker will also have to manage the complex array of investigations that Democrats plan to launch into Trump, his family, and cabinet members now that they have regained control of the chamber's oversight powers.

Rounding out their party leadership team, House Democrats also elected Katherine Clark of Massachusetts as majority leader, Pete Aguilar of California as majority whip, and Joe Neguse of Colorado as assistant speaker.

#65. Ghana's new $60m presidential jet sparks public outrage

The French-made Falcon 6X replaces the ageing presidential jet, officials say

Ghana's government has defended its purchase of a new presidential jet amid criticism over its cost, as many citizens continue to struggle with the high cost of living.

The $60m (Â£46m) French-made Falcon 6X has sparked a political row, with opposition politicians accusing President John Mahama's administration of misplaced priorities.

But the government says the purchase is justified, arguing that Ghana's improving economy makes the aircraft affordable and is needed to replace an ageing presidential jet.

The controversy comes as Mahama's administration seeks to demonstrate an economic recovery after years of financial difficulties that forced Ghana to seek IMF assistance.

Can Ghana's new president meet the voters' high expectations?

Manufactured by French aerospace company Dassault Aviation, the aircraft was delivered to the Ghana Air Force on Monday at a reported cost of $61m.

Parliament approved its purchase last December as part of a broader programme to modernise Ghana's military aviation fleet, which also includes four helicopters from Airbus.

The jet was welcomed with a water salute on Monday, but its arrival triggered criticism online and among opposition politicians over the cost and timing of the purchase.

While in the opposition, Mahama himself opposed buying a new presidential jet, prompting questions about why his administration has now gone head with the purchase.

Opposition MP Samuel Abu Jinapor questioned the government's priorities, saying the expenditure came at a time when many Ghanaians continue to struggle with the cost of living and youth unemployment remained alarmingly high.

"What was unacceptable in opposition cannot suddenly become acceptable in government merely because the occupants of power have changed," Jinapor added.

He accused the governing National Democratic Congress (NDC) of hypocrisy, recalling how its senior figures had strongly opposed proposals for a new presidential aircraft when they were in opposition.

Jinapor also called for greater transparency over the procurement process, how the purchase was financed and the government's justification for the expenditure.

Defence Minister Kenneth Adjei welcomed and inspected the new jet on Monday

The purchase has also prompted sharp criticism on social media, with some users arguing the money could have been spent on more pressing public needs.

"This money can employ over 100,000 nurses and 200,000 teachers," an X user posted.

But the government has rejected the criticism, saying Ghana's economic circumstances have changed considerably since the NDC opposed a similar purchase.

Peter Lanchene Toobu, chairman of parliament's defence committee, said the party had never opposed acquiring a presidential jet in principle.

Its objection, he said, had been on the timing when Ghana was experiencing severe economic difficulties.

"We agreed in principle that we needed to purchase a new presidential jet," he said.

He argued, however, that Ghana could not justify the expenditure at a time when it was seeking a $3bn bailout from the IMF.

"The strength and macroeconomic stability of this country have enhanced our status in the sense that imports have become cheaper, and this is the right time," he added.

Toobu said the government had purchased the aircraft without borrowing, citing a stronger Ghanaian currency and improved economic conditions.

Government spokesperson Kwakye Ofosu also dismissed suggestions that the purchase was a misplaced priority.

He pointed to falling prices of construction materials and lower borrowing costs as evidence of the country's improved economic situation.

"When I was campaigning in 2024, and I was donating cement, it cost 115 cedis (Â£7.5) a bag. Today, for 80 cedis, you will get a bag of cement," he told JoyNews.

The controversy has also extended to the aircraft's interior, following claims that it has a dedicated bedroom and bathroom.

But Ofosu dismissed claims that the jet had such luxury facilities, saying the president has no intention of "bathing in the air".

"There certainly is no bathroom. President Mahama has no intention of bathing in the air," Ofosu said.

He explained that the jet has reclining seats that can be converted into sleeping arrangements but denied that it has a dedicated bedroom.

The government says the new jet will replace the 16-year-old Falcon 900EX, which has suffered recurring maintenance problems, including corrosion and leakages in its fuel tank.

The older jet was reportedly grounded in France for several months in 2025 while undergoing repairs.

Officials say the Falcon 6X has a longer flying range, reducing the need for refuelling stops on international trips and improving the reliability of presidential travel.

Professor Godfred Alufar Bokpin, an economist at the University of Ghana said that while the president's safety was paramount, the timing of the purchase was difficult to justify amid severe austerity measures, high unemployment and widespread infrastructure challenges.

"The timing makes it a bit difficult to understand," he told the BBC, adding that the government should provide a cost-benefit analysis explaining why buying the jet was preferable to leasing one and how the decision serves Ghana's interests.

Additional reporting by Thomas Naadi in Accra

#64. Apec summit as it happened: Xi welcomes Trump on China visit as Putin claims Europe in ‘humiliating decline’

This blog is now closed, you can read more on this story here

The Associated Press is reporting that Donald Trump plans to hold a meeting with Vladimir Putin on Thursday, citing an anonymous US official. The meeting would be in addition to the blockbuster trilateral talks the two will hold with host Xi Jinping on Thursday.

Meanwhile, a meeting between the leaders of China, the United States and Russia planned for Thursday in Shenzhen is set to last less than 60 minutes, Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told Russian state media on Wednesday. “No more than an hour” has been set aside for the historic summit between Xi Jinping, Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin, Peskov was quoted as saying by Russian state news agencies.

Xi Jinping told Donald Trump he hoped their talks would help advance a “constructive, strategic and stable relationship” between China and the US, based on “respect, fairness and reciprocity”, according to China’s state news agency Xinhua. Xi also recalled Trump’s personal welcome at the airport during his visit to the US in September, saying China’s own welcome for the US president reflected its tradition of reciprocity.

Xi personally greeted Trump on the airport tarmac in Shenzhen, reciprocating the welcome the US president extended to the Chinese leader during his visit to the US in September.

Before landing in Shenzhen, Donald Trump enjoyed lunch in Manila with Philippine president Ferdinand Marcos Jr at the Malacañang presidential palace. Speaking during a meeting ahead of lunch, Marcos revealed that he had arranged for fried chicken from Filipino fast food chain Jollibee to be delivered to the US delegation as a late-night snack last night, praising Trump for “working very hard”.

Chinese state media have released a readout of Xi Jinping’s earlier meeting with Vladimir Putin. In it, Xi is quoted as saying China-Russia relations are “unshaken by storms and steady as a mountain”, and have set “an example of a new type of major-country relationship characterised by equality, mutual respect, trustworthiness, and win-win co-operation”.

Canada’s Mark Carney has hit back at Vladimir Putin’s earlier assertion that sanctions no longer work in this day and age. “Mr Putin can ask his financial institutions, he can ask his oligarch friends, he can ask his hockey players whether or not sanctions are a thing of the past. I think he’ll find out that they’re very real, they’re biting,” Carney told reporters.

Mexican president Claudia Sheinbaum won praise and went viral on Chinese social media on Wednesday for taking an economy flight to attend a regional summit in Shenzhen. World leaders this week descended on the southern Chinese city to attend the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation meetings – many arriving in specially chartered commercial airliners or dedicated government aircraft like US president Donald Trump’s Air Force One.

It is unclear whether North Korean leader Kim Jong-un will attend the talks, but Trump has floated the possibility. On Wednesday US secretary of state Marco Rubio said the US president was willing to engage, but he did not confirm whether a meeting had been set in Shenzhen.

Trump said on Wednesday that the US has just ⁠concluded a “very big ⁠trade ​deal” with the Philippines, but he did not provide any details, and Manila ⁠did not announce any agreement. “We’ve just consummated a very big trade deal, the ⁠biggest trade deal we’ve ever made so far, and it ​is a fair ‌deal for both countries,” ‌Trump said during a state luncheon at the Philippine ‌presidential palace. Trump is set to arrive in Shenzhen on Wednesday evening.

Trump landed in China on Wednesday seeking to reinforce America’s status as the world’s leading technology power, but a distressing series of technical outages back home has left his administration scrambling to calm the US public.

The issues put the administration on the defensive at a moment when the president wants to project strength and certainty before the meeting with Xi and Putin.

As he began his trip to Asia, Trump initially had been nonchalant about the outages that left people in the US worried about accessing their email, checking their bank accounts or safely flying on an airplane.

“It’s fine,” Trump said Tuesday from the Philippine capital of Manila, suggesting it was all just a consequence of being a nation that is “big and powerful.”

The meeting between the leaders of China, the United States and Russia planned for Thursday in Shenzhen is set to last less than 60 minutes, Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told Russian state media.

“No more than an hour” has been set aside for the historic summit between Xi Jinping, Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin, Peskov was quoted as saying by Russian state news agencies.

The Associated Press is reporting that Donald Trump plans to hold a separate meeting with Vladimir Putin on Thursday, citing an anonymous US official.

The meeting would be in addition to the blockbuster trilateral talks the two will hold with host Xi Jinping on Thursday.

If confirmed, it would be their first bilateral since they met in Alaska in 2025 for talks that failed to reach a deal on the war in Ukraine.

Trump had already suggested he might meet with Putin, as well as with North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, but neither option had been added to his official schedule.

#57. Kuwait says it repelled first Iranian attack in two months after US strikes island

Kuwait condemned the attack, which Iran said targeted an airbase hosting US forces (file photo)

Kuwait and Bahrain have said they repelled Iranian air attacks overnight as hostilities between Iran and the United States rise.

The drone attack on Kuwait was the first in more than two months. The Iranian army said it had targeted the Ali Al Salem Airbase, which hosts US forces, but Kuwaiti authorities did not report any damage.

It came after US warplanes struck an Iranian missile launch site on Qeshm Island in the Strait of Hormuz, according to US military officials.

Meanwhile, the United Arab Emirates' state oil firm said a crew member was missing after one of its tankers was attacked in the Gulf waterway on Tuesday.

The recent exchanges of fire, as well as deadlock in negotiations on a peace deal, have raised fears of a major escalation in the nine-month-old war.

The conflict began when the US and Israel attacked Iran in late February.

Since then, Iran has retaliated for US and Israeli strikes by launching missiles and drones at Israel and Gulf Arab states hosting US military assets and personnel.

It has also attacked dozens of commercial vessels in and around the Strait of Hormuz, through which a fifth of global oil and gas shipments usually pass.

Pakistani man sentenced to 27 years for Iran-backed plot to kill Trump

Trump says US taking 'very seriously' any Iranian plot against Washington

Iran targets US military base in Bahrain with drone attack

On Tuesday night, Kuwait's military said its air defences engaged "drone threats" but did not report any casualties or damage.

The Kuwaiti foreign ministry condemned the attack, saying it "flagrantly violated Kuwait's sovereignty, directly threatened its security and stability, and constituted a grave breach of international law".

"The explosions seemed to be coming from far away, but hearing them again after a period of calm was both surprising and worrying," Hani Mohammed, an Egyptian resident of Kuwait, told AFP news agency.

Bahrain's military also said on Wednesday that its air defence systems had "intercepted and destroyed several Iranian aerial attacks".

The island kingdom, which hosts the US Navy's 5th Fleet, has reported four attacks over the past six days, following a two-month lull.

Qatar, which has mediated in the stalled indirect talks between Iran and the US, said the Iranian attacks on its neighbours were a "dangerous escalation that threatens to complicate efforts aimed at containing tensions".

The Iranian army said its drones had targeted US Patriot air defence systems and drone and missile launch platforms at Ali Al Salem Air Base in Kuwait.

Later, the army's commander-in-chief, Maj Gen Amir Hatami, warned the US and its allies that it would respond more forcefully to any new attacks on Iran.

"If the enemy repeats its mistake... we have the readiness and the plans to confront it and its supporters in a stronger, more crushing way and without geographic limitations," he said.

On Tuesday night, US fighter jets targeted a missile launch site on Qeshm Island "with precision munitions" in response to Iranian "aggression targeting US personnel and assets", US military officials said.

Iranian media reported explosions on the island but cited a local official as saying they had caused no damage to infrastructure.

In a separate development, the Abu Dhabi National Oil Company (Adnoc) said a search-and-rescue operation was under way for a missing sailor following an attack on one of its vessels in the Strait of Hormuz on Tuesday. The rest of the crew were safely evacuated and 11 were receiving medical care, it added.

The UK Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) centre reported that a tanker was struck by an unknown projectile about 13 nautical miles (24km) north of Khasab, in Oman's Musandam peninsula. It said the attack caused a fire on board that was extinguished.

Iran said two oil tankers and a ship carrying liquefied natural gas (LNG) had caught fire after hitting naval mines while sailing along an "authorised route".

At least 25 seafarers have been killed across the Middle East since the start of the war, when the Strait of Hormuz was effectively closed due to Iranian missile and drone attacks on commercial vessels and a US naval blockade of Iranian ports.

The US and Iran reached a preliminary agreement to end the conflict and reopen the strait in June, but it collapsed within weeks after Iranian attacks on shipping resumed and the US reinstated its blockade.

Iran insisted it would not give up some measure of control over the waterway and began targeting vessels using a southern route through Omani waters that avoids its preferred route through Iranian waters.

In recent months, the US military has been providing protection for tankers shuttling through the Strait of Hormuz, allowing crude oil exports from Gulf states to reach around 80% of pre-war levels, according to analysts.

#56. Datacentres to create 70% more carbon emissions by 2030, IEA report warns

Global energy watchdog forecasts huge rise as US and China turn to fossil fuels to power AI growth agendas

The world’s datacentres will create 70% more carbon emissions within the next four years as the US and China turn to fossil fuels to power their AI growth agendas, according to the world’s energy watchdog.

The International Energy Agency (IEA) warned that datacentres will consume twice as much electricity by 2030 compared with today, with the global expansion of artificial intelligence accounting for three-quarters of this growth.

The Paris-based agency found that the breakneck pace of the industry’s growth means that within four years AI datacentres will consume an extra 350 terrawatt-hours of electricity every year, or more than the annual electricity consumption of Great Britain.

Its influential annual report warned that the world’s energy future was less certain after the impact of the Middle East war, which it has described as the greatest energy market disruption in history.

“I don’t see any credible pathway for the energy world to return to the way it was before,” said the IEA’s executive director Fatih Birol.

Instead, it will be reshaped by the policy decisions governments make after the crisis to meet the increasing demand for energy to power a growing global population, the rising need for air-conditioning and a booming AI economy, the IEA said.

Datacentre developers have emerged as the world’s biggest corporate buyers of renewable energy to help fuel their servers which have a connection to local power grids. Despite this, the report showed that the appetite for fossil fuels is expected to grow even faster, particularly in the US and China.

By the end of the decade, around half of the electricity needed to power China’s AI datacentres will come from coal power plants, according to the IEA. The rest will come from renewable energy, with smaller amounts from nuclear reactors and very little from gas plants.

In contrast, the US is expected to rely on gas to provide about 60% of the electricity for its datacentres by 2030. There were plans for about 180GW of new gas-fired power plants linked to US datacentres in August this year, the agency said. That is almost as much new gas capacity as the total now installed across the European Union.

As a result of the growth in fossil fuel generation for datacentres, emissions associated with this sector were on track to rise by 70% by 2030, the IEA said.

The appetite for gas power among US datacentre developers is more than 16 times higher than in Canada, where the IEA identified plans for 11GW of gas generation to power their servers. In the rest of the world, AI developers have put forward plans for new gas plants with a combined capacity of just 4GW, the agency said.

This is because renewable energy is expected to play a greater role in supplying datacentres outside the US and China. In the EU, the IEA expects datacentres to rely on renewables for about 70% of their electricity by 2030 – followed by nuclear power, gas generation, and a very small amount of coal power.

The agency based its forecasts for the notoriously opaque industry on data showing the annual global shipment of AI servers, which have climbed eighteen-fold since 2020. It also used space-based imagery to track the buildout of 93 large “AI factories” around the world.

Its analysis suggests that the world’s datacentre capacity has climbed tenfold in the last 24 months to 17GW. By the time the AI factories were completed, their combined capacity was likely to be about 50GW, the IEA said.

The world’s existing stock of datacentres, including those with AI-optimised servers, now stands at 120GW, with 80% of this capacity in China, the US and the EU. A further 120GW will be built by the end of the decade under the IEA scenario often considered its central base case, of which three-quarters will be AI datacentres.

#52. William and Kate meet PM Modi as they wrap up India tour

The Prince and Princess of Wales have met India's prime minister as they ended their three-day visit to the country.

Narendra Modi thanked Prince William for bringing his Earthshot prize to the country during the couple's first international tour since 2022.

It comes a day after the royal couple gave out Â£1m prizes to winners of the environmental award, which was founded by the prince and remains one of his biggest personal projects.

Catherine also visited a maternity hospital, where she comforted a grieving mother who lost a baby after giving birth to premature twin girls.

Street cricket with a legend then metro ride as William and Kate begin India visit

On Wednesday, William and Catherine travelled from Mumbai to capital New Delhi for the final leg of their trip.

During a meeting at Modi's private residence, a Kensington Palace spokesperson said the royals and Modi discussed the "strength and importance of the UKâIndia bilateral relationship, reflecting on the deep and enduring ties between the two countries and the opportunities to strengthen their partnership in the years ahead".

The prince and princess also spoke "warmly" of their time in the country, thanking the prime minister and the people for their welcome, and saying how much they enjoyed returning to India, a decade after their last visit.

Modi called it a "pleasure" to meet them, and said he looks forward to "further strengthening" the UKâIndia partnership for the "shared prosperity of the people of our nations and the wider world".

When Prince William announced earlier this year that The Earthshot Prize ceremony was going to be held in India for the first time, he highlighted India's leadership in climate innovation, its biodiversity and its massive youth population as the key reasons for choosing the country.

The royal spokesperson said that the meeting with Modi touched on the success of the ceremony in Mumbai, and India's "growing leadership in environmental innovation".

The event, founded by Prince William in 2020, was hosted this year by Karan Johar, presenter of The Traitors India and one of the country's most well-known filmmakers.

The couple last met the Indian prime minister on their official visit to India in 2016.

Watch: Catherine visited a maternity hospital in Mumbai on the last day of royal visit

Ahead of their meeting with Modi on Wednesday, William met with film stars during a visit to a film production company, while Catherine visited a maternity hospital, where she met grieving mother Jyoti Karande.

Karande, whose other child remains in intensive care, is receiving treatment in the post-partum ward at Nowrosjee Wadia Maternity Hospital.

Catherine told the 29-year-old: "Stay strong. Well done. You've clearly been very, very brave."

William and Catherine's first official overseas trip in four years was short, but allowed them to combine personal projects - like Earthshot - with diplomacy.

As the future king and queen, they are viewed as a valuable diplomatic asset by the UK government.

And with Catherine recovering well and back on international duties following her cancer treatment, visits of a similar kind can be expected to fill their calendar.

#51. Eight-year-old Haitian boy returns home after release from ICE custody

Child reunited with family after he was detained by immigration agents in San Diego and transferred to Texas

An eight-year-old Haitian boy who was put into restraints and detained by immigration agents in San Diego on 4 November and transferred to a facility in Texas has been reunited with his family in California.

Representative Juan Vargas shared a photograph of the boy and his family in a social media post on Monda, writing: “11 days ago, an 8-year-old in San Diego was taken into federal custody during an immigration check-in. He was separated from his loved ones and held in a facility in Texas. Today, he’s finally home.”

The Haitian Bridge Alliance, an immigration advocacy group, confirmed that the third-grader had been released from Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) custody.

“An eight-year-old child belongs in a classroom, on a playground, and surrounded by love – not in immigration detention. We welcome his release, but his freedom should never have required a public outcry,” Guerline Jozef, executive director of the Haitian Bridge Alliance, said in a statement.

The third-grader at Fay elementary school was put in restraints and taken into custody by federal immigration officers at a check-in earlier in the month, according to video obtained by NBC News and an oversight letter Democratic lawmakers sent to the Departments of Homeland Security and Health and Human Services.

A spokesperson for the homeland security department said ICE took the child into custody “out of an abundance of caution and to ensure the child’s safety”.

The uncle, the spokesperson said in a statement to the Guardian, was unable to prove legal guardianship – and that officials were obliged by federal anti-trafficking laws to take the boy into care as an “unaccompanied alien child”.

The Haitian Bridge Alliance said the boy was in the care of his uncle and cousins after his father was deported last year.

“I’m so grateful that he’s home, but this should have never happened in the first place,” Vargas said in a second social media post, adding that he would be asking the Trump administration for answers. While he was detained, the child was “held in a facility in Texas, far away from his home in San Diego”, Vargas said.

Celebrating the boy’s return, Jozef of the Haitian Bridge Alliance told NBC News: “The aunt was literally crying, and she said: ‘I have not eaten since the Wednesday they took my baby.’ They haven’t been able to sleep because of that.” She added: “The cousins were there when they took him, and wondering: will they ever see him again? But today [Monday] they are able to see him again. They are able to hug him.”
